- **The 2026-09-19 deep backfill, and what it bought.** Run as Phase 0 of the trend-only backtest: 177 symbols (the `/quality` page's own thin list, verified to contain none of the 196 deep ones), at the default depth 5000. **640,433 bars written, 0 failed, 0 retried.** The archive went **1,125,447 → 1,708,406 rows** and its earliest bar moved from 2006-10-12 to **2003-01-09** — three years further back than the file had ever recorded, because several long-listed names had only ever had one shallow price pull.

| reaches back | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| 3 years | 260 | **406** |
| 5 years | 253 | **392** |
| 10 years | 211 | **332** |
| 15 years | 183 | **297** |
| 19 years | 169 | **276** |

  `/quality`'s own flags moved with it: `noFiveYear` **166 → 27**, clean **250 → 389**, thin **177 → 38**. **Only 12 stocks now hold under 320 sessions and every one is a genuine recent listing** (CBRS from 2026-05-14, SKHY 2026-07-10, GOOGM/GOOGN 2026-06-03…), so there is nothing left for a backfill to fix — the remaining shallowness is the market's, not the archive's.
  - **Use the default depth, not the page's command, for a deep study.** `/quality`'s fix command says `--depth 1300` — five years, which is what its own "no 5Y" flag is about. A nineteen-year study wants the default 5000.
  - **The dry run is the cheap way to find out what is actually available**, and it corrected a wrong inference: "164 stocks under 320 sessions were never backfilled" was only half right. CBRS returns 88 bars because it listed in May 2026, while VSAT returns the full 5000 back to 2006. One credit a symbol, six seconds for 177, and it reports the real first date per symbol before anything is written.
- **Never run a shallow `--depth` over symbols that already have deep history** — each symbol is replaced wholesale, so `--depth 1300` would truncate twenty years to five. Use `--only` with the shallow ones (2026-09-15: 190 of 271 had under 1,300 bars).
- **`backfill-bars.js` is a local script, not a route.** 5000 bars is ~580 KB per symbol and ~40 MB across the universe: fine locally, far past what a serverless function should hold. Same one-off pattern as `migrate-to-turso.js`, dry-run by default.
- **Writes are incremental, not wholesale.** `persistBars()` upserts only bars newer than the stored `max(d)` plus a `BAR_OVERLAP` of 5. Steady state is a handful of rows per symbol per refresh, not 300.
- **The overlap is not decoration.** Twelve Data returns a bar for *today* while the market is open, with the current price as its close, so a mid-session refresh stores a provisional value. Re-upserting the recent window replaces it with the settled close. Measured drift on a real pull: 0.003–0.007%.
- **Splits are detected, not ignored.** A split re-prices all of history, so a stored bar `SPLIT_PROBE_BARS` (60) back would silently disagree with the fetched one and leave a phantom cliff in any chart. `persistBars()` compares that one bar per symbol — one query for the whole universe, since US symbols share trading days — and rewrites the symbol in full when it differs by more than `SPLIT_TOLERANCE` (0.5%). Re-running `backfill-bars.js --only SYM` is the manual repair.
- **An as-of pull must never write.** `computeStocks(asOf)` fetches a truncated range; persisting from it would corrupt the archive. Guarded by `if (!asOf)`, the same rule the snapshot uses.
- **A failed archive write never fails the refresh** — it is caught and logged. The screener is the product; the archive is a by-product.
- `open` is stored although nothing reads it yet. `high`/`low` drive the 52-week range and `volume` the volume trend, so an archive without them could draw a chart but not reproduce the screener — which is the point of keeping it.

## The nightly job
`.github/workflows/nightly-refresh.yml` runs a Refresh all at **4:15 PM in New York** — fifteen minutes after the close (changed 2026-09-15; it was 00:00 UTC, which drifted between 8PM and 7PM Eastern).

**A local time needs two crons and a guard, because GitHub cron is UTC and has no timezone option.** Both UTC times are registered — **20:15 while EDT is in force and 21:15 while EST is** — and the first step of the job converts `now` to `America/New_York` and stops unless the hour is 16. Exactly one of the pair can be right on any given day. **The guard asks the tz database rather than hardcoding changeover dates**, because those move; the US has shifted them before. Verified by replaying 400 days through the real tz data: exactly one run fires per day, with clean handover on 2026-03-08 (21:15Z → 20:15Z) and 2026-11-01 (back again). A `workflow_dispatch` run is never gated.

**4:15 PM is close to the close, and that is a deliberate trade.** Twelve Data returns a bar for *today* while the market is open whose close is the current price, so a run this soon after the bell can store a value that is not yet the settled close. It self-heals: `persistBars()` re-upserts a `BAR_OVERLAP` window of 5 sessions, so the next day's run overwrites it with the final figure (measured drift on a real pull: 0.003-0.007%). What it costs is that the evening's snapshot may carry a provisional close. Moving to 4:30 or 5:00 PM is one digit in both cron lines if that ever matters.

**A runner, not a Vercel cron, because a full pass takes ~13 minutes** and no serverless function stays alive that long. The workflow drives exactly the loop the browser drives.

- **`POST /api/cron/refresh`** is the job's only endpoint, authenticated by `Authorization: Bearer $CRON_SECRET`. Deliberately **not** admin: the secret satisfies that one route, so a leak cannot delete a portfolio. Unset leaves the route closed, not open. `?start=1` expires the cache and raises the flag; every later call runs one round. `DELETE` on the same path ends a run early and reports what it got.
- **The 62-second gap between rounds is not conservatism — it is measured.** Two rounds inside one minute returned *"1128 API credits were used, with the current limit being 610"*. A round costs roughly **560 credits** — and the decomposition was re-measured off `Api-Credits-Request` on 2026-09-13: **profiles are 85% of it, not the prices.** Exact per-call costs: `time_series` **1 credit per symbol** (batched or single), `/statistics` **50**, `/profile` **10**, `/earnings` (8 quarters) **20** — a cold profile is **80 credits/symbol**, so 6 per round = 480, plus ~1/symbol for the batch. The plan ceiling of 610/minute allows at most **7 cold profiles a minute**; prices for even a 300-symbol universe would cost only ~300 credits. Don't lower `GAP` without measuring again.
- The job stops after 3 rounds without progress, same as the browser loop, and cleans up the flag on its way out.
- Repository secrets required: `APP_URL`, `CRON_SECRET`.

### The refresh report
**Both kinds of refresh email the owner** — a Refresh all and an ordinary price Refresh — **whoever started it**, the job or the button. The Refresh all path hangs off `endRefresh()` rather than the cron route, because both ways of finishing one converge there.

- **Which report goes out is decided by whether a run was live a moment earlier, not by what the `delete` returned.** `endRefresh()` runs either way so a flag left stale by an abandoned run gets cleared, but a *stalled* Refresh all still reports nothing, and the click that happens to complete the universe is reported as the ordinary Refresh it was.
- **An ordinary Refresh has no `refresh_state` row**, so `/api/stocks` passes its own `startedAt` and actor into `finishLiveRefresh()` as `ctx`. No `ctx`, no plain report — which is what keeps the nightly job's dozen rounds quiet.
- **The plain report says `Fundamentals: not re-pulled — prices only`.** A price Refresh reuses cached profiles, so presenting the fundamentals as fresh would make the mail actively misleading. It is judged on failed symbols alone; coverage is a Refresh all's measure, and "no profile yet" is omitted entirely.
- **A plain price Refresh no longer mails (2026-09-15)** — manual or scheduled. Intraday refreshes made it thirteen emails a trading day, burying the Refresh all report that matters. `sendRefreshReport(state, 'plain')` still builds the report and stores it on the run for `/refreshes`; only `kind !== 'plain'` resolves a recipient. Refresh all, Fill missing and the nightly job mail as before.

- **Receipt**: run time first and largest, then the start and finish clock times, actor, `n/total` coverage, failed symbols, symbols still without a profile, prices-as-of, fundamentals rows recorded, bar-archive size and through-date. The duration leads because it is the number that says whether the night went normally — a Refresh all that finishes in seconds did not really run.
- **Clock times are rendered in `REPORT_TZ`, defaulting to `America/New_York`** — the runner is UTC and the reader is not. `fmtClock()` uses explicit `month`/`day`/`hour` components rather than `dateStyle`/`timeStyle`, because ECMA-402 refuses to combine those with `timeZoneName` and the throw lands in the fallback, which would quietly print UTC instead of saying so.
- **The digest is gone, and "The run" replaced it (2026-09-16, owner's call).** The day's movers and the advice changes both restated a standing the screener shows live, and neither said anything about the job — which is what this mail is for. In their place, the figures from the Refresh runs page: **rounds** (and how many were refused), **profiles** pulled and refused, **credits** with a per-round average against the 610/min ceiling, whether **prices** were pulled live or read from the archive, **round time** (average and longest), **round spacing** (against the 62s floor), the **trigger**, a link to that run's page and, for a nightly, the Actions job log. `runFacts()` reads the run's own row and its rounds. Removing the advice section also took the per-symbol news read off the report path — it no longer touches the `news` table.
- **The final round is now recorded BEFORE the tail runs.** `finishLiveRefresh` is what closes the run and builds the report, so a round recorded after it was missing from the very summary it completed; the counts it needs are derived from the same rows a moment earlier.
- **A Fast refresh mails as "Fast refresh"** — subject and heading both, from `state.mode`, rather than borrowing Refresh all's name.
- Verified end to end with the provider and the mail API stubbed: a Refresh all and a Fast refresh each driven to completion, then the delivered body checked — no movers, no advice changes, the run section present with rounds/credits/profiles/timing, the run link, the receipt still leading with the duration, and each job named correctly in its own subject and heading (18 checks).
- *Removed:* the day's five best and worst movers. **The screens and the highest-rated list were removed in Sep 2026** — both restated a *standing* rather than reporting what happened, both are one click away on `/analysis` and the screener where they are current rather than frozen at whenever the refresh finished, and together they were most of the email. `Screens.run()` over the whole universe went with them, so the report costs less to build too. `REPORT_SCREEN_NAMES`, `rating()` and `symLink()` were deleted as dead code; `Screens` is still imported for `cleanWeights` and `PAST_PERIODS`.
- **Fundamentals that moved** — *Refresh all only*, since only a Refresh all re-pulls the profile cache; a plain Refresh would be comparing a number with itself. Each symbol's row in `fundamentals_history` is compared against **the previous recorded set**, which is not "yesterday": a row exists only for days a Refresh all actually ran, so after a quiet stretch the comparison reaches further back. The email prints that date, because a change "since yesterday" that is really since last Thursday is a lie told by omission. `readFundamentalsPair(day)` fetches both sets in one round trip — measured at **48ms** against Turso.
  - **Six fields are deliberately excluded**: price, market cap, forward P/E, PEG, FCF yield and net-cash %. Measured across the recorded history, each moves on **79–100% of consecutive nights with a median of about 2%**, because each is divided by, or is, the price. They restate the day's price move, which the movers list already gives. The twelve fields that *are* tracked move on **1–5% of nights**, and when they move it is a **step, not a wobble** — AVGO's revenue sat at 75.46B for five recorded days, went to 89.10B, and stayed. The email says which fields are left out and why, so their absence is not read as an omission.
  - **Two derived signals were tried and rejected.** Forward EPS revision, backed out as `relΔ(price) − relΔ(forward P/E)`, and share-count change as `relΔ(market cap) − relΔ(price)`. Both look clever and both measure the wrong thing: SNOW threw a "+20.9% EPS revision" on a day its forward P/E was **byte-identical**, and the share-count signal oscillated −20.9% then +22.1% on consecutive days. The profile blob and the price are not sampled at the same instant, so the subtraction reads that staleness rather than the company.
  - **Absolutes are compared relatively, percentages in points.** A margin going from 0.1% to 0.3% is "+200%" and means nothing; it is +0.2pt. Thresholds are **5%** for an absolute (with a 1M floor, below which a percentage is silly) and **1 point** for a percentage — sized against the recorded history to yield roughly seven field-moves a night, clustered into one or two companies.
  - **A sign change is worded, not percentaged.** Net income going −29M → 59M is not "+302%", it is a company that started making money, and it leads the section. `netIncomeTtm`, `fcfTtm` and `netCash` each carry their own pair of phrases ("turned profitable" / "swung to a loss", "moved to net cash" / "moved to net debt").
  - **A point move's rank is capped** (`FUND_PTS_SCORE_CAP`), because a percentage-point change is not on the same scale as a relative one and the growth rates swing wildly — AVGO's earnings growth moved **128.6 points** on the night its net income rose 31%, and uncapped the derived figure outranked the report it came from.
  - **A symbol with no earlier row is counted, not compared** — it is new to the archive, not a company that changed overnight. The night the 33 faders were added, the email would have said "33 recorded for the first time, so nothing to compare." (They have since been pruned back to 14 — see the universe section.)
  - Values are printed **currency-neutral** (`89.10B`, not `$89.10B`): these are in the company's reporting currency, and Samsung's revenue is not dollars.
- A run that *stalls* sends nothing — nobody calls `endRefresh()` and the flag ages out on its own. The cron job covers that case, since it is the thing still awake.
- `REPORT_TO` overrides the destination; unset it falls back to the owner account's email, so it works before anything is configured.
- **Fundamentals rows are dated by `marketDay(rows)`** — the freshest bar in the universe, not the server clock. This mattered more at the old 8PM Eastern slot, where the server's UTC date was already tomorrow and dating by `new Date()` filed every automated run a day ahead of its own bars. At 4:15 PM Eastern the UTC date still agrees, but the rule stands: the bars decide the date, not the clock.

## Chatbot
`/chat` answers questions about the screener data. Open to **any signed-in user** (`requireAuth`), with the allowance set by role.

**The whole dataset goes in the system prompt — there is no retrieval layer, and adding one would make it worse.** Measured against the live API, not estimated: the system prompt was **~17,500 tokens** for 69 stocks, roughly 250 per stock — so at the current 93 it is around **23,000**, and worth re-measuring off `usage` rather than scaling this number if it ever matters. (A bytes/4 estimate said 7,400 — it underestimates dense numeric CSV by well over 2x, so trust `usage` in a real response, not arithmetic on the payload.) That still fits a 200k context about eleven times over and stays viable past 700 stocks, well beyond the 119-ticker ceiling the price API imposes. Embeddings are the wrong tool twice: the data is a numeric table where "margin above 15%" is an exact filter rather than a similarity, and retrieval could only drop rows the answer needs.

- **`cache_control` sits on the data block, which caches everything before it too** — so the rules block rides along and the whole system prompt is cached. A real call showed `cache_read_input_tokens: 17470` against `input_tokens: 31`: after the first question, all you pay for is the question and the answer.
- **`max_tokens` must cover the model's reasoning, not just the reply.** At 1200 a ranking question spent the entire budget thinking and came back with a single `thinking` block and no text at all — a silent empty answer. It is 6000 now. The reasoning is worth paying for: working through 69 rows is exactly where a model miscounts. `stop_reason === 'max_tokens'` is reported to the user as "ask something narrower" rather than a generic failure.
- **`CHAT_FIELDS` is the glossary and the column list at once.** Adding a column to the bot means adding one entry there — nothing else. Fields absent from older snapshots simply come through blank.
- **Derived values are computed server-side on purpose** (`fcfYield`, `netCashPct`, all three margins, `range52Pos`). Asking a model to divide across 69 rows is where it goes wrong, so the prompt tells it to use the column rather than re-derive it.
- **Four behavioural rules live in `chatRules()`**, and they are the actual product: name the specific gap and redirect rather than refusing bare; general finance and company knowledge is fine but flagged as background; no buy/sell/hold verdicts (declined for the same reason as any unsupported claim, *not* as a standing disclaimer); "will it go up" is unknowable rather than missing. The prompt lists what the data does **not** contain — a general "say you don't know" instruction does not stop confabulation, an explicit inventory does.
- **Quota lives in `chat_usage`** (one row per user per UTC day), because an in-memory counter cannot work when consecutive requests need not share a process. It is charged only after the config and validation checks, so a malformed request cannot burn it, and the 429 is returned before any API call — a member over their limit costs nothing.
- **Two ceilings:** `CHAT_DAILY_LIMIT` (60) for the owner, `CHAT_DAILY_LIMIT_MEMBER` (3) for everyone else. The owner pays for the key and does the prompt tuning; members get enough to be useful. **Keyed on the account**, so a shared login shares the allowance rather than multiplying it. With the current roster that caps daily exposure at 60 + 3 per member.
- **Upstream errors are never echoed to the client** — the response body can restate the request, and the API key travels in the same headers.
- Not streaming yet. Responses are a few seconds behind a "thinking" indicator; streaming is the obvious next step but could not be verified against Vercel from here.
- `private/chat.html` carries a ~60-line markdown renderer for the reply (tables, lists, headings, bold/italic/code). **It escapes HTML before applying any markdown**, so nothing a model returns can inject markup.

**Gated pages are no longer served raw** — see **Serving and the gate** above. The short version: they left `public/`, because nothing in `public/` can be protected.

## Does any of this predict anything? — the research log
**Read this before designing another backtest.** Several framings have been tested against the full archive and all of them came back flat. The point of writing them down is so the next session tries something new rather than rediscovering the same negatives.

**The momentum half of this log moved to [docs/momentum-delta.md](docs/momentum-delta.md) on 2026-09-15**, when momentum over time was retired from the product — the delta, the stored history, the signal study and the lab's slope indicator. Five framings on the momentum score and its delta are recorded there, every one flat, the strongest reading **r = −0.003** against the next fortnight. Read it before proposing anything momentum-shaped; do not re-run those tests from memory.

**The owner's constraint: a maximum holding period of one to two months.** That is the hardest horizon there is — short-term reversal has faded and momentum has not started — and it rules out most of what the literature offers. Anything proposed should be judged against it.

### What has been tested, and what came back

| test | result |
|---|---|
| **range position → next month**, three definitions (60d z-score, 252d and 120d high-low band), cross-sectional | **Flat, and mildly the wrong way.** Bottom band −0.00% to +0.53%, top band +0.23% to +0.27%; largest \|t\| anywhere 1.2 in one thin cell. The *top* of the range does slightly better than the bottom |
| **the two falling-knife filters** on the bottom band — idiosyncratic vs market-wide fall, and "has it turned" | Neither helps; both point slightly the wrong way. Stacking trend + idiosyncratic + turned leaves **under 500 observations in nineteen years**, ~26 a year — unusable even if it worked |
| **RSI**, 40 settings, cross-sectional at one month | **Best top-decile t: 1.76** on the 116-symbol universe, **2.73** on the 93-symbol one — see the universe section; the pool changed, not the indicator. |
| | Pre-2020 t 0.1, post-2020 t 2.2. Pre-2020 t 0.1, post-2020 t 2.2. On a single stock the deciles are visibly non-flat while the correlation reads 0.044 — which is the lesson, not a finding |
| **risk-adjusted velocity**, 144 parameter settings, cross-sectional at one month | **Best top-decile t anywhere on the grid: 1.73.** With 144 tests that is noise. Pre-2020 t 0.2–0.6, post-2020 t 1.6–2.1 — the same split as everything else |
| **a 50-day-only trend rule**, raw and with 2/3-close persistence filters, vs the 200-day rule, 90 symbols | **Worse than the 200D at every cost level, in both eras.** Median 158 round trips vs 62 (persistence trims to 68 but does not rescue returns); beats the 200D on 30/90 stocks at zero cost, 19/90 at 20 bps; identical drawdown medians, so it buys nothing on risk either. The faster re-boarding is real (PTON: −12% vs the 200D's −62%) and swamped by churn everywhere else (NVDA: +4,446% vs +47,355%). Measured 2026-09-12; do not build a 50D mode without a new reason |
| **ranking WITHIN an advice tier by room to exit** (2026-09-17; 307,965 stock-days, 42,285 in the technical Strong Buy tier, 3,463 days where 4+ competed, ranked within the day against the universe's equal-weight return) | **The raw distance orders the downside BACKWARDS.** The roomiest half of a tier sits 25.6% above its 200-day on 31.7% volatility against 13.8% and 24.3% — "more room" is "more extended" — and its tail is fatter in 6 of 6 era/horizon cells (3M p10 −18.5% vs −15.7%). Divided by the stock's own volatility it inverts and behaves: thinner p10 AND p25 in 6 of 6 cells, both eras, by 0.9–3.3 points. **Neither version picks winners** — every mean-excess difference is noise (|t| ≤ 1.6 raw, ≤ 0.6 adjusted) and the raw one's sign flips post-2020. Shipped as the **Cushion** column and the advice-sort tiebreak, labelled as tail size |
| **the all-technical verdict itself, swept over twenty years** (2026-09-20; `/trend-backtest` on the complete `tech_history` — 222 monthly start dates 2008→2026, 3M horizon, Strong Buy + Buy, each basket against its own universe's equal-weight return that window) | **Flat to slightly negative, and it holds through the 2020 split.** Median excess **−0.32%**, hit rate **44.6%**, on 74 independent readings; pre-2020 −0.27% / 47.2%, post-2020 −0.89% / 39.7%. The seventh framing to come back flat. Caveat that weakens it as a test: the tier qualifies **108 of 336** stocks, so the basket is nearly the benchmark and there is little room for selection to show — the discriminating version (Strong Buy alone, or a top-N cut inside the tier) is untested |
| **volume confirmation on breakouts** (2026-09-14; three definitions × vol/20d-avg at 1.25/1.5/2.0×, benchmark-relative 1M/2M excess) | **The first result whose sign survives the 2020 split.** Fresh 3M-high crosses on ≥1.5× volume beat quiet ones in BOTH eras (pre-2020 +0.36%/1M t 2.56, +0.56%/2M t 2.52; post +0.88% t 2.43, +1.39% t 2.54; pooled t 3.4–3.6 on 32k events); 52W-high crosses agree, strongest pre-2020 (t 3.2–3.5). Event clustering means the honest t is nearer 2–2.5. **The 200D upcross gets nothing from volume in either era** — the engine's trend gate has no volume story. Effect is real-looking but small (+½–1% per event over 1–2 months) and not monotone (2× is weaker than 1.5× — blowoff days). Suggestive; fit for a descriptive marker, not a gate |

### The conclusions

- **The momentum delta was not predictive in any framing**, and in September 2026 it was removed from the product along with everything built on it — see [docs/momentum-delta.md](docs/momentum-delta.md). The live score stays, as a description of where a stock stands rather than a claim about where it is going.
- **The range hypothesis does not hold here.** "Stocks trade in ranges, buy the lower side, avoid the falling knife" was tested with three range definitions and both knife filters. Every cell sits inside noise, and the faint direction runs *toward* momentum rather than reversion. The one cell that looked interesting — bottom band, trend intact, not falling worse than the market — reads **−0.03% before 2020 against +1.59% after, t 0.6**: the same regime split as everything else.
- **The trend filter (above/below the 200-day) is the only thing that keeps reappearing**, positive in four of five range bands and negative below the line in four of five. It is never significant (|t| ≤ 0.8) and `from_high` looked this promising once too, so treat it as a lead and not a finding.
- **A sixth framing came back flat on return, and one came back inverted.** Ranking inside an advice tier by room to exit looks like a safety measure and is an extension-and-volatility measure; the roomiest names had the worst tails. Volatility-adjusting it fixes the ordering of the tail and changes nothing about the mean. The lesson is the `vol trend` one again: **a risk field that is not normalised by the stock's own volatility is measuring size, not risk.**
- **Nothing yet clears a bar worth acting on**, least of all at a one-to-two-month horizon. The nearest miss is volume-confirmed momentum breakouts (above), the only line whose sign held through the 2020 split — worth showing as a fact beside the Entry state, not worth gating a rule on.
- **The screener is descriptive rather than predictive**, and that is a legitimate thing for it to be: a consistent way to see where every stock in the universe stands and how each got there.

### The method, which matters more than any single result

Four ways to make a weak relationship look strong, each of which has caught something real here:

- **Hold out a period before looking.** `from_high` looked like the one factor with a consistent sign across the whole archive until it was re-run on the widened universe, where it fell from t −2.2 to −1.4 and lost half its magnitude. It was a universe artefact.
- **Discount for overlapping windows.** Daily observations of a 10-day forward return carry about one observation's worth of evidence per ten. A level is far more autocorrelated than a delta, so its effective sample is smaller still — a per-stock test of the level has almost no power, whatever its n says.
- **Rank within the day, not across the pool.** Both legs then live through the same fortnight, so the market subtracts out and the baseline becomes zero by construction. This is the only framing that has shown anything at all.
- **Give a hit rate its base rate.** A stock that rises most fortnights hands a high hit rate to a signal that knows nothing. Only the lift is worth reading. Splitting a 0-100 score at zero once reported a lift of exactly `+0.0 pts` for every horizon — an artefact that looked like a finding.

### What would actually change the answer

Five hypotheses have now come back flat, which starts to say something about the search space rather than the hypotheses. The binding constraints, in order:

- **The universe.** 93 large, heavily-covered, correlated names is the most arbitraged corner of the market, and at a one-month horizon most of their variance is common. Mean reversion in particular works better in higher-volatility, less-followed stocks. Changing this would do more than any signal.
- **The data.** Everything tested comes from daily bars. Earnings revisions, short interest, options positioning and intraday behaviour are all absent, and they are where the remaining short-horizon effects live.
- **The horizon.** One to two months is the gap between reversal and momentum. Nothing much lives there.

### Untested, and worth doing

- **Post-earnings announcement drift** is the best fit for a one-to-two-month hold that exists — right timescale, durable literature, and there is already a "Drifting after a beat" screen built on the idea. **Recording began 2026-09-13**: `next_earnings_date`, `next_earnings_estimated`, `last_earnings_date` and `last_surprise` now ride every Refresh-all row of `fundamentals_history` (via `FUND_EXTRAS` in db.js — written beside `FUND_FIELDS`, deliberately outside the email's numeric moved-fields pipeline). The study becomes answerable a couple of quarters after that date; nothing before it is recoverable.
- **Benchmark-relative returns everywhere** — subtract the universe's equal-weight return that day. It turns "did it go up" into "did it beat its peers", which is the only version that survives the remaining bias.
- **Longer horizons, 6m and 12m.** The model is built around 12-1 momentum and has never been tested at the horizon it was designed for. Two more `LEAD`s in the view.
- **A per-factor sign.** `cleanWeights()` clamps weights to `0…MAX_WEIGHT` as integers, so a factor can be zeroed but not inverted. Expressing a reversion model needs a sign. Build that as a second model, not as a preset — a momentum model with its momentum inverted is a different thing wearing the same name.

### Where the analysis actually runs

**On the owner's machine, in throwaway scripts, leaving no trace.** None of the numbers above can be reproduced from the product; there is no button for any of it. If a result starts mattering, precompute it nightly into its own table the way `fundamentals_history` is — a page that recomputes a universe-wide statistic per request would be the 3.8s cross-sectional query on every load.

**The wall is a duration, not a size.** An early note here said "~36 MB in one round trip" and that was wrong — measured properly:

| query | result |
|---|---|
| `readBarsFor`-shaped, 650 days x universe — **the biggest the app issues** | **50,036 rows, 2.0s** |
| one symbol's full momentum history | 4,734 rows, 0.2s |
| the snapshot `/api/stocks` serves | 0.34 MB |
| all bars, `close` only | 441,483 rows, **82.6s — ok** |
| all bars, every column | 441,483 rows, **158.1s — ok** |
| both of those in parallel | **fails at 215.8s**, having read 36.2 MB |
| `ntile()` deciles aggregated server-side, returning 10 rows | **ok, but 159.7s** |

So a single query has carried 441k full rows fine, and the failure came after **83 MB**. The boundary sits between **158s and 216s** — a response timeout of roughly three minutes somewhere in the stack. Parallel queries fail *sooner in bytes* only because they share wall-clock, which is what made it look like a size cap.

- **Production is nowhere near it.** The largest query any route issues is 50k rows in 2.0s — about eighty times inside the wall. This constrains ad-hoc analysis, nothing else.
- **Pushing the work into SQL is not the escape hatch it looks like.** The `ntile()` aggregation returns ten rows and still took 159.7s: for whole-archive work Turso is compute-bound too, and that query is itself close to timing out.
- **For repeated analysis, use the local copy — `analysis-db.js`.** `node:sqlite` ships with Node 24, so it adds no dependency. **Full build 48.6s for 852,706 rows; incremental sync 4.7s.** Verified faithful on creation: every table's count matched and 726 spot-checked values differed by exactly zero.
  - **The gain is not marginal.** The `ntile()` decile query takes **0.53s locally against 159.7s on Turso — 300x**, and the whole momentum table loads into memory in 0.39s against 41s chunked over the network. Research that was minutes-per-iteration is now instant, and costs no rows against the Turso account.
  - **It deliberately omits `users`, `sessions` and `password_resets`** — password hashes and live session tokens have no business in an unencrypted file on a laptop — and `visitors`, `prefs`, `chat_usage` as personal and useless here. **It is not a backup.**
  - The schema is copied from `sqlite_master` rather than restated, so a column added upstream arrives without this file knowing about it. Dated tables re-pull `OVERLAP_DAYS` (10) on top of what is local, because the archive rewrites recent bars — a provisional close for a session still in progress is not final. A split rewrites a symbol's whole history, so **`--full` after a split**, the same repair path `backfill-bars.js --only` is for.

## The strategy backtest
`/strategy` is the first thing here that simulates a rule rather than measuring an association: signal, position, rebalance, equity curve. Reached from **Strategy** in the screener's bar. **The word "backtest" was freed for it** — what used to carry that name is now the forward-returns view.

**Why a separate page from the lab.** The lab is per-symbol and asks whether an indicator is associated with what happens next. A portfolio is not about a symbol, and its outputs — equity curve, Sharpe, drawdown, turnover, correlation to holding — fit none of the lab's panels. Its controls are different too: sizing, rebalancing, filters, costs.

- **The rule is Moskowitz–Ooi–Pedersen time-series momentum**: long what rose over the lookback, short what fell, each position sized `targetVol ÷ its own realised vol`, rebalanced monthly, the last month of the lookback skipped. Stock selection is a dropdown over **All plus every portfolio**, so a rule can be run on Mag 7 or on the Faded 33.
- **The day is earned before the rebalance, and that was a bug first.** Rebalancing first decided a weight from today's close and then earned today's move with it. The sizing was the worse half — `vol[i]` contains the very return being earned, so a rule facing a gap sized itself down for a move it could not have seen. Measured on a synthetic series with a 48% gap landing on a rebalance day: **the wrong order booked 5.98%, the right one books 39.98%.** Note the direction — here the lookahead *understated* the return, which is why "does it look too good" is not a sufficient test for one.
- **Monthly returns, turnover and average exposure are shipped, not finished statistics.** That is what makes cost, target volatility and the cash rate sliders rather than assumptions: cost is charged against real turnover, vol scaling is linear in the returns, and the cash credit follows exposure — so all three apply after the fact. At a monthly rebalance across a hundred names, cost is often the difference between a positive and a negative result. Monthly rather than daily is what keeps the payload manageable; it costs drawdown resolution, and the page says so.
- **One file per universe, plus an index (`strategy-index.json`).** The grid is 192 variants and every universe's copy of it is ~13 MB — a multi-megabyte download to read one entry in a dropdown. The page fetches the index, then the chosen universe, and caches each. The largest is ~950 KB raw, **~230 KB over the wire**, and only one is ever fetched. Size tracks the 192 variants x 225 months, not the number of symbols, so pruning the universe barely moves it.
- **The grid covers six dimensions**: lookback (21/63/126/252), direction, the 200-day filter, **leverage cap (1× / 3×)**, **rebalance (daily / weekly / monthly)** and **volatility window (20 / 60)**. The last three were added after a piece of published long-only research specified 1×, daily and 20 — three things the page could not express. `skip` (21) and the stored `targetVol` (10) stay fixed.
- **Idle cash earns, and that is a slider.** A long-only rule behind a 200-day filter sits in cash for much of its life; crediting it nothing understates it by whatever bills paid. The rate is credited on `1 − exposure` (capped at 1, so a levered book earns nothing on cash) and buy-and-hold gets none, being always fully invested. An **Invested** card reports average gross exposure, because that single number explains a modest CAGR better than anything else on the page.
- **The cash rate is also subtracted as the risk-free rate in Sharpe, and skipping that half was a bug.** Crediting 4% on a book sitting 87% in cash and *not* subtracting it reported **Sharpe 2.79** — the T-bill's ratio, borrowed. Sharpe is excess of cash by definition. Both the strategy and the benchmark take the same rate, or the comparison is rigged. The invariant that proves it: for a fully-uninvested book the Sharpe is **−0.09 with or without the credit**, since adding a constant and then measuring excess over it changes nothing. A `s > 1e-12` guard returns `null` rather than a ratio for a constant series, which otherwise printed **3.45 for a T-bill** out of floating-point residue.
- **"Match buy-and-hold" exists because a comparison at different exposures is meaningless.** The stored runs sit at ~29% gross exposure, so their raw CAGR looks terrible against a fully-invested benchmark while their drawdown looks wonderful. Neither number says anything until the volatilities match.
- **A gap is carried forward for at most `MAX_CARRY` (10) sessions; a longer hole, and a symbol not yet listed, stay null.** Without *any* carry a single missing print reads as a 100% loss and back again. Without the **bound**, GPS — which has a **1,373-session hole between 2019-11 and 2025-03** — was held flat at $16.78 for five and a half years: realised volatility of exactly zero, so `targetVol ÷ vol` produced a weight of **3,227,535×** and the leverage cap silently *became* the position; it then earned nothing for the whole stretch and booked the entire re-listing as one **+33% day**. A hole is absent data, not a quiet stock. 10 is chosen to clear a foreign listing's holiday calendar and nothing more — 005930 (Samsung) legitimately misses up to 6 consecutive US sessions over Chuseok, and is the only other symbol in the archive with an internal gap over one.
- **`MIN_VOL` (1% annualised) floors the divisor** as a second guard, so the failure is impossible rather than merely unlikely. After both fixes the largest raw weight anywhere is **7.83× on VRT**, a genuinely quiet stretch, which is what the cap is for.
- **The leverage cap barely binds in this universe, and the control says so.** At a 10% target these stocks are volatile enough that `target ÷ vol` lands under 1× on **99.2% of stock-days**, so 1× and 3× agree to within 0.1 points of CAGR. It would bite in a calmer universe, or at a higher target — but note that target volatility is applied afterwards as a multiplier, so **raising it does not re-apply the cap**. The cap belongs to the 10% the grid was run at, and the hint says that too.
- **The first result on this project that does not vanish before 2020.** At matched volatility, 5 bps, nothing earned on cash, **on the 116-symbol universe**: CAGR **+16.7% against hold's +18.6%**, Sharpe **0.97 against 0.95**, max drawdown **−28.1% against −43.4%** — and in **2008–2019 alone, Sharpe 0.95 against hold's 0.84**. Re-measured on the 93-symbol universe it reads **+18.8% against +21.8%**, Sharpe **1.09 against 1.03**, max drawdown **−22.1% against −44.1%**. It earns that by sidestepping 2008, which is visible in the curve and is mechanically what a trend rule does. It is a *risk* result, not a return result, and it remains hostage to a universe with no bankruptcies in it.
- **The published long-only formulation beats holding, once the SMA bug above is fixed.** Set as specified — 252-day lookback, mandatory 200-day filter, 1× cap, daily, 20-day vol — at matched volatility and 5 bps: CAGR **+14.8%** against hold's +18.6%, Sharpe **1.00 against 0.95**, max drawdown **−18.2% against −43.4%**. Credit 4% on the cash it holds and subtract the same 4% from both Sharpes and it is **0.80 against 0.75**, ahead in *both* halves (0.63 vs 0.61 pre-2020, 1.04 vs 0.96 after). **This is the corrected reading**: the first pass reported 0.66 against 0.75 and "does not beat holding", which was `smaSeries` excluding 49 symbols rather than a property of the rule.
  - **All of the above is on the 116-symbol universe. On the 93-symbol one the "both halves" claim no longer holds**: Sharpe 0.86 against hold's 0.84 over the whole period, but **0.66 against 0.69 pre-2020** — it loses the half that matters. Same code, same settings, 19 fewer laggards. Treat the pre-2020 half as the test and this as a reminder of how little separates the two answers. The `Set the published long-only rule` link on the page puts all five knobs there in one click.
- **Daily rebalancing is only optimal below about 3 bps.** At matched volatility the spec rule turns over **199% of capital a month** against 55% monthly. Measured crossovers: daily leads weekly to **2.77 bps**, weekly leads monthly to **15.26 bps**, monthly above that. Research quoting a daily-rebalanced vol-scaled rule at zero cost is quoting the most flattering version of the most expensive setting — which is why cost and cash yield are both on screen, since they pull opposite ways and omitting both is not neutral.
- **`smaSeries` returned all nulls for any series with a leading null, and that silently broke every `+200D` result.** A symbol that listed after the window's start has leading nulls; the function bailed at the first one and returned an empty series, which then failed the `sma[i] == null` guard on every session — so **49 of 116 symbols held no position at all in any 200-day-filter variant**, while still counting in the divisor. The filter was not being selective, it was disqualifying nearly half the universe. Fixed to restart the window on a hole rather than end the series; **113 of 116 now get a usable average** (the other three are too short). Every `+200D` number published before 2026-09-10 was wrong, including the first read on the long-only spec, and the correction *reversed* its conclusion.

## The single-stock strategy
`/single` runs the same trend rule on **one stock at a time** — not which stock to own, but whether to own the one you have picked, and how much of it. Reached from **Single** in the screener's bar, beside Strategy.

**It is an exposure control, not a stock picker**, and that changes what counts as success. The benchmark is holding that same stock, and the honest expectation is that it loses on return and wins on drawdown, because a rule that sits in cash part of the time must give up upside to avoid downside.

- **Nothing is precomputed — the page ships the prices and simulates in the browser.** That is the opposite of `/strategy`, and the reason is a correctness one: **the position cap is a nonlinearity.** `/strategy` stores runs at a 10% target and scales afterwards, which is exact for everything except the cap, so raising the target there does *not* re-apply it. The cap binds on 0.8% of stock-days at a 10% target and **10.8% at 15%**, so the published rule's 1× cap at 15% is a setting that page cannot honestly express. Recomputing from prices makes every slider exact.
- **The engine is `strategy.js`, unchanged.** One symbol is a universe of one. Two options were added for this page and both default off, so the stored portfolio grid keeps its meaning: `exitOnSignal` (rebalance the day the signal flips, not only on the schedule) and `opts.ppy` (periods per year — 252 here, 12 for the monthly series `/strategy` ships). A single stock is small enough to measure daily, so its drawdown is the real intra-period low rather than a month-end understatement.
- **`single-closes.json` is ~700 KB gzipped at 93 symbols (888 KB at 116), and the precision was measured rather than assumed.** The archive holds split-adjusted floats, so essentially every symbol carries more than two decimals. Rounding to cents corrupts a daily return by up to **5.45 percentage points** — NVDA's 2007 closes are $0.16 after every split since, where a cent is 3% of the price. 4dp costs 750 KB and is wrong by at most 0.007 points of CAGR; the shipped 1e6 scale costs 888 KB and is exact. Deltas rather than prices, because consecutive closes are close together and small integers compress.
- **Both legs start where the rule first becomes evaluable.** Comparing a strategy's post-warm-up years against a benchmark's whole history is how a backtest flatters itself for free.
- **"Match buy-and-hold" solves for the target volatility rather than scaling a result**, by bisection, because the cap makes the relationship non-linear. It is load-bearing: at the defaults the rule averages **29% invested** against a fully-invested benchmark, so the CAGR column is not a fair fight and the page says so. **At a 1× cap the rule often cannot reach the stock's volatility at all** — it saturates, which the note explains rather than leaving the link looking broken. Matched at 3×: AAPL **25.1% against 24.2%** (Sharpe 0.76 vs 0.74), MU **45.7% against 30.1%** (0.89 vs 0.68).
- **Sharpe is the fair column and CAGR is not.** Sharpe is return over volatility, so it barely moves when the rule runs at a fraction of the exposure — the exposure cancels, including the cash credit and its matching risk-free subtraction. The verdict line says which to read.
- **The sweep is the point of the page.** The identical rule at the identical settings across the whole universe, because one stock rebalanced monthly over eighteen years is perhaps twenty independent decisions, and the tickers this gets tested on are the ones that went up. **~90 simulations in ~180ms**, so it follows a slider drag on a 140ms debounce rather than being a build step. `runOne(symbol, full)` skips slicing the chart-sized arrays the sweep would throw away.
- **What it says at the published settings: beats holding on Sharpe in 22 of 87 stocks (25%)**, cuts the worst drawdown in all 87 (which is arithmetic, not skill — anything holding cash does that), median time invested 28%. It was 33 of 110 (30%) on the 116-symbol universe. On the **Faded 33** the median CAGR gap turns *positive* (+1.7%), which is the one hint of something: a trend rule earns its keep on stocks that fall, and those are exactly the names a survivor-only universe under-represents.
- **A hole longer than `MAX_CARRY` (10) sessions is left blank**, the same bound `/strategy` uses and for the same reason recorded there.

## The indicator lab
`/lab/<SYMBOL>` builds a short-horizon indicator, draws it under the price, and reports what it does — for that stock live, and for the whole universe from a precomputed grid. Reached from **Indicator lab** beside Signal study on the stock page.

**Three indicators so far.** `skip` appears in both and is 12-1's trick at a short horizon: the recent days are where reversal lives, so leaving them out asks for continuation without buying the bounce.

- **Risk-adjusted velocity** — a short-horizon return divided by the stock's own volatility, filling the hole in the momentum score, which risk-adjusts at 3, 6 and 12 months and nothing below. Knobs: lookback, skip, vol window, smoothing.
- **Momentum score slope** — the least-squares slope of the score, in points per session. Knobs: fit window, skip, smoothing. **No volatility normaliser, and that is not an oversight**: the score has been on a fixed absolute scale since September, so 0.4 points a session means the same thing for every stock — which it would not while the score was a percentile. A direct payoff from that change.
  - **`compute(series, params, id)` takes the whole bundle** `{ closes, score, dates }` rather than closes, because the two indicators read different data with different start dates. Threading two signatures through three surfaces would have been worse than one bundle everywhere.
- **RSI** — Wilder's plain reading, not the sub-score the model derives from it. Knobs: period, skip, smoothing. Verified against `Momentum.rsiSeriesAt` at **gap 0.00e+0**, and against the screener's own RSI column (61.1603 both).

- **`indicators.js` is shared three ways** — the page, the server and the offline grid — for the same reason `momentum.js` and `screens.js` are. A second implementation of "vol-normalised 10-day return" would have drifted inside a week. Verified against `momentum.js` on creation: `windowReturn` agrees to **0.00e+0** and `realisedVolSeries` to **1.14e-13** once the parameterisations are lined up (momentum.js takes two endpoints, this takes length-and-skip, so its 252→21 is lookback 231 skip 21).
- **Series are oldest-first here**, the reverse of `momentum.js`, because charts and rolling windows both read forward. Flip once at the boundary rather than reasoning about a reversed index in every loop.
- **The rolling volatility requires its full window, which deliberately differs from `momentum.js`.** That function scores one date from whatever history it has; this one draws a line, and a line whose early points used 20 returns and whose later ones used 126 is not one measurement along its length.
- **The forward horizon is selectable — 1 week, 2 weeks, 1 month, 2 months, 3 months — and nothing is hardcoded to 21.** `HORIZONS` lives in `indicators.js` because that is the module all three surfaces load, and a page offering a horizon the grid never computed would snap silently to the wrong row.
  - **No forward-return array is shipped.** The browser has the closes, so `forwardReturn()` derives it for whichever horizon is chosen. Sending one array per horizon would have been five copies of something the page can work out, and it is the only way an arbitrary horizon could work at all.
  - **The overlap discount follows the horizon**, which is the point of showing it: MU's 4,638 usable sessions are **939 independent observations at a week and 73 at three months**. A three-month test looks like it has thousands of points and has seventy-three.
  - The grid covers **every indicator × every horizon** — 1,160 rows, five horizons × 232 settings — and snaps to the nearest computed horizon the same way the parameters snap, saying so when it does.
- **The browser computes the indicator, not the server.** A round trip per slider drag would make the control feel like a query. `/api/lab` ships bars and forward returns; the page recomputes the indicator and every statistic through the same modules the server uses. Redraws are debounced 90ms while the number beside the slider updates immediately.
- **The decile panel is the one that matters for a non-monotonic indicator**, and it is why RSI was worth adding. A correlation measures a *straight line*, so a relationship where both ends pay and the middle does not cancels itself out and reports zero — which is exactly the shape RSI is supposed to have, and why the model's own `rsiScore()` is a curve rather than a slope. Live demonstration on MU: **correlation 0.0440, which reads as nothing, while decile 9 returns +7.49% against a +3.46% overall mean.** Bars are measured against the overall mean rather than zero, because zero only tells you the stock went up. Buckets come from the lab's own bucketing.
- **The scatter colours the dots by era**, blue before 2020 and cyan after, with a fit line for each and the two correlations in the legend. The regime split every result on this project has shown is the thing to look for, and a reader should see two populations rather than consult a card. Clicking a legend chip hides that era; hiding both is refused, since an empty chart is not a view of anything.
- **A full recompute redraws both charts in 28ms** for MU's 4,680 sessions, comfortably inside the 90ms debounce. Dot radius steps down above 1,200 and 2,500 points, `dotNodes` is captured once per render, and the middle-99% axis with strays pinned in amber exists because: a handful of extreme fortnights would otherwise press every other point into a band a few pixels tall.
- **The page is a machine for finding things that are not there, and is built to say so.** Four sliders over one stock is thousands of combinations and some will look excellent by chance. So the **universe-wide result and the pre-2020 / post-2020 split sit on screen beside whatever is being tuned**, and the caption names the best |t| anywhere on the grid so a promising-looking cell can be compared against it.
- **`lab-grid.js` calls `compute()` rather than composing the indicator itself.** The first version inlined the composition so it could cache volatility across parameter sets — a second implementation of the indicator, which is the exact thing `indicators.js` exists to prevent, and untenable the moment there were two. The caching bought seconds in a script that runs offline. One grid per indicator now, over that indicator's own knobs.
- **`lab-grid.json` is committed derived data.** 192 settings across two indicators, 60 KB, rebuilt by `lab-grid.js` in 28.1s against the local copy. Shipping it static means the page costs the server nothing; the page prints the build date so a stale grid is visible rather than silent. Re-run it after a backfill.
- **Log price axis above a 4x range**, the same rule the price chart follows — MU ran from $70 to $1,033 over three years and a linear axis flattened all but the last few months onto the floor.
- **What the grid says so far: nothing, for any of the three.** RSI's best top-decile t is **2.73** across 40 settings (it was 1.76 before the universe was pruned — the pool got easier, the indicator did not get better) — and note that is the *cross-sectional* view, where the per-stock decile shape above largely washes out.
- **What the grid says: nothing, for either of the first two.** Velocity's best top-decile t over the full period is **2.76** across 144 settings and the slope's is **0.87** across 48 — **1.73 and 0.80 respectively before the universe was pruned.** A number that moves that far because 19 laggards left is a number about the sample. With that many tests anything under about 3 is noise, and the split is the familiar one — the slope reads pre-2020 t −1.3, post-2020 t +2.0. **A better-estimated version of a flat thing is still flat**, which is the useful answer: the delta's problem was never its estimator.
- **`num()` returned 0 for null**, because `Number(null)` is 0 and finite. It never showed while the only input was closes, which are never null — the moment the score arrived, with 273 blank sessions before a symbol is scoreable, slopes were being fitted from a fabricated zero up to the first real reading. Caught by a count that was impossible: 4,988 slope values from 4,734 scores. **Reject the empties before coercing, not after**, and check counts against what the input can support.

## The help page
`/help` explains the app to the people using it, so it is open to **any signed-in user** rather than admin-only. Reached from `Help` in the ⋯ menu, beside Contact, and from a link in the Weights menu itself — which is where the question actually arises.

- **Sixteen numbered sections behind a contents grid**: what the app is, reading the table, how scoring works, the eight momentum factors, presets and Custom, the Quality score, screens, the stock page, the assistant, data freshness, the account, the Advice column, **tiles, news, the phone page and cards to share**. Each ends with a link back to the contents, and `scroll-margin-top` clears the sticky island on an anchor jump.
- **It is the manual, and there is deliberately only one (2026-09-17).** The owner asked for a user manual; `/help` already was one — signed-in rather than admin, reached from `Help` in the ⋯ menu — so the four features written after it were added to it rather than to a second page. A separate guide beside it would have drifted the way every other duplicated thing here has. The gaps were tiles (the switch, per-row, the shared range, and that Tile setup is site-wide), news (the column's two kinds of blank, the panel, the 12-hour ticker, Check now), the phone page (the four tabs, views, the chart toggle, and that a reload is what fetches new data), and cards (Download PNG at true size, and saved posts remembering controls rather than a picture). Three older sections were missing their back-to-contents link and got one. **No new links were added anywhere — the owner's call.** Verified in a browser: 16 contents entries resolving to 16 sections in agreeing order, every section reachable back, anchor jumps clearing the sticky island, no sideways scroll at 390px, no text under 12px.
- Written for someone with a hand on a control, not someone studying the model — every factor says what raising or lowering it changes, not only what it is. Owner-only features carry an `.adm` badge rather than being hidden, so members can see what the buttons they lack actually do.
- **It restates numbers that live in code** — the default weights, the 65/35 blend, the preset table. If those change, this page has to change with them; there is deliberately no attempt to generate it from `screens.js`, because the prose around each number matters more than the number.
- The caution about fitting a weighting to today's list lives at the bottom of the presets section, next to the thing that invites it.

## Momentum weight lens
**The editor lives on `/admin`; `/` and `/analysis` read it.** `/analysis` still carries its own Default · Trend · Steady selector above the screens; the screener shows `#lensChip` when the weighting is not Default and nothing otherwise. Each page keeps its own choice; the nightly report, the assistant and the stored snapshot always stay on Default, so the shared score remains one comparable measurement.

- **On the screener the lens reaches further, so it is marked loudly**: it changes Momentum and Overall, which means it also changes the sort. `body.lensed` tints those columns (`.lens-col`) and the picker in accent. Quality is deliberately left plain — it is the one score a momentum weighting does not change. Marking was a per-cell bottom rule at first, which drew a line between every pair of rows and read as a ladder rather than a column.
- **The CSV names the weighting in the column headers it alters** — `Overall (Trend)`, `Momentum (Trend)` — and in the filename. A comment row would have changed the shape every parser expects; a header rename travels with the file and leaves the column order alone, which the export is documented to keep stable.
- **`applyWeights()` restates the weights inside `momentumBreakdown` too**, or the factor tooltip would explain a score using percentages that did not produce it.
- **`renderWeightMenu()` carries its own escaper.** The only `esc` in `index.html` is scoped inside the CSV export; reaching for it there throws and takes `render()` down with it — this has now caused two bugs, so check the scope before using that name on that page.

It re-scores momentum **in the browser** — the screener, the nightly report, the assistant and the stored snapshot all stay on Default, so the shared score remains one comparable measurement.

- **It costs nothing to compute.** `momentumBreakdown` already ships every factor's sub-score to the client, so re-weighting is arithmetic on data that is already there: no refetch, no endpoint, no API credits. `applyWeights()` returns a shallow copy using the same field names, so screens and cell renderers need no idea a lens is in play.
- **`momentumBreakdownPrev` ships too**, or the change column would re-weight today and not the fortnight-ago comparison and quietly mix two schemes.
- **Presets key on a stable `key` per factor, not the caption.** Labels are prose and may be reworded; `key` is the contract.
- **Weights need not total 100** — `scoreWithWeights()` renormalises over whichever factors have data, mirroring `scoreFactors()`.
- **The axis is continuation versus caution**, not adjectives. Trend leans short-horizon, strengthens trend regime and removes the reversal brake entirely; Steady leans on 12-1 and consistency and keeps it.
- **The bar prints how far the weighting actually moves the list** — measured live *on the 82-symbol universe*: Trend reordered 69 of 82 names, median 4 places, largest 20; Steady 75 of 82. The universe is 116 now, so those figures are stale as absolutes; the bar computes them live and is not. A preset that reordered three names would be a dial that does nothing, and this says so rather than hiding it.
- **`Custom` adds sliders, edited in one place only** — the **Scoring** section of `/admin` since 2026-09-14, the screener's Weights menu before that. `/analysis` offers Custom as a fourth choice and reads the saved set, but never edits it: two editors would double the work and drift.
  - **`cleanWeights()` in `screens.js` is used by both the browser and the server.** Only the eight known factor keys survive, as integers in `0…MAX_WEIGHT`, and an all-zero map is rejected because it leaves nothing to score with. That is what keeps `PUT /api/prefs` from becoming free per-user storage — verified: `evil`, `__proto__` and a negative are all dropped.
  - **The menu is built on open, not on every render.** A rebuild mid-drag replaces the slider under the cursor and kills the gesture, so `render()` redraws only the trigger.
  - **Table redraws are debounced 120ms** while the numbers beside each slider update immediately. A full re-score and rebuild is ~80ms, far too slow to run on every step of a drag.
  - **Each slider shows its renormalised share** (`25 → 23%`) because weights need not total 100, so a bare number means nothing on its own.
  - **Any page that writes prefs must hand back the parts it does not edit.** `/analysis` sending `collapsed: {}` silently reset every collapsed column group — it now returns what it was given.
- **The saved choice is a preset id plus, for Custom only, the map behind it.** A bad id falls back to Default.

## The Advice column
**Displayed as "Advice" since 2026-09-12** (renamed from "Action" at the owner's instruction; earlier candidates Verdict/Stance/Call were passed over). **Every internal identifier keeps the old name** — group id `act`, `grp-act` classes, `s.action`/`actionFlag`/`actionTrend` fields, `ActionRules`, the `action` prefs id — the Scores/`rank` precedent, so saved prefs, code and tests stay stable while the words changed. The CSV headers are now `Advice` / `Advice Reason`; Help §12 opens with a one-line disclaimer ("a mechanical reading of the table, not a recommendation tailored to you"), which was the price of the friendlier word.

**A change since the previous trading session is marked** — a chevron before the Advice word (the momentum-arrow language, other side), tooltip and hover card naming yesterday's verdict. **The amber bar on the row's Symbol cell went on 2026-09-16** (owner: "I don't need that anymore as we have the changed filter now on top") — the Changed chip filters to exactly those rows and the chevron still marks which verdict moved, so the bar was a third way of saying the same thing. `adviceMoved()` stays; only the `chgRow` class and its rule are gone, and both refresh emails carry an "Advice changes" section between the movers and the fundamentals (Balanced rules, biggest tier jumps first, "none — every verdict held" when quiet). Mechanics: `computeStocks()` stores `prevTech` on each row (the engine's technical inputs one bar back — fundamentals are day-cached steps and stand for both days), `scoreActionInto()` re-evaluates it as `advicePrev`, and the client's `scoreActionRows()` does the same under the active profile so the marker follows the picker. **The indicator is empty until the first refresh after deploy stores prevTech.** Dry-run measured 12 changes across 93 rows on a normal day.

An **Advice** column group (formerly Action Model) of its own (id `act`, `#5eead4`, mirrored in rowcard.js and analysis.html like every group): `Type · Trend · Entry · Fund. · Guards · Action`, plus a per-cell tooltip on Action naming **the one rule that fired**. The four state columns are the intermediate computations made visible — each condition family collapsed to ONE word (`states()` in action.js), priority-ordered like the rules, so the derivation reads left to right: Trend answers *may*, Entry answers *now*, Fundamentals answer *conviction*, Guards answer *wait*. Guards is blank on ~95% of rows by design — a quiet column is one that means something when it speaks. Sorting the state columns ranks by exported severity ladders (`TREND_ORDER` etc.), not the alphabet. Colour only on danger words (Breakdown, Downtrend, Extended, Weak); the Action stays the one loud cell. This is the second build; the first (a points model) was reverted, and the owner's constraints for the redo are load-bearing: **no custom composites by default** (Momentum, Quality — `use_momentum` / `use_quality` restore the original gates), no MA Cross, loss-avoidance first.

- **First-match-wins rule lists, not scoring.** Each type has an ordered list; the Sell/Avoid/Hold rules sit above every Buy rule **in the source**, and config can move thresholds but never reorder the lists. That is the loss-avoidance guarantee, and it is why every verdict has exactly one nameable reason.
- **Blanks satisfy no comparison in either direction** — and that is why the "No trend data" rule exists: without it a young listing with a blank 200-day fell through every trend rule to a blind Buy with Risk. Second closed hole: Weak fundamentals block Buy with Risk above the 200D. Both fixes are toggleable (`fixes.*`) and the off state reproduces the source brief exactly.
- **Early is scored on different rules, not different thresholds** — growth, gross margin, burn, short interest; never P/E or earnings growth. ETFs (detected by tag or by having no Quality *and* no forward P/E) get the technical rules alone — which is also what makes the backtest honest: an all-technical row classifies as ETF, so `action-backtest.js` replays the real engine, no fork.
- **Backtest verdict, RE-MEASURED on 2026-09-20 over 1,588,936 stock-days** — five times the sample the line below was written on, after two corrections to `action-backtest.js` and the deep backfill. **The conclusion is unchanged**: the tiers order **downside** correctly and the medians are flat.

| 3M, all years | p10 | median |
|---|---|---|
| Strong Buy | −13.1% | +3.0% |
| Buy | −13.8% | +3.1% |
| Hold | −17.3% | +3.3% |
| Avoid | −16.6% | +3.4% |
| Sell Immediately | −27.7% | **+4.5%** |

  Holds in both eras (pre-2020 −12.1% → −28.4%; post-2020 −14.5% → −27.1%). **Sell Immediately still has the HIGHEST median** and the fattest tail, which is the original reading on five times the data: the model manages risk and does not pick winners. Avoid sits marginally *better* than Hold on the tail, so the ordering is not monotone through the middle.
  - **Two stale assumptions were found in the script, both silent.** It read `select distinct symbol from theme_tickers` — which has not been the universe since the `universe` table was introduced, because a theme only GROUPS stocks. Measured: **241 symbols against 430 with bars**, so the eighteen-year study had been running on a little over half the screener. It reads `bars` now, the same source `build-tech-history.js` uses, since the local analysis copy does not sync `universe` and does not need to. And its survivorship footnote said "these **93** all still trade" long after the universe reached 430 — a hardcoded number in the one sentence whose job is to stop the reader trusting the table. It is counted now.
- *(superseded — the measurement above replaces it)* **Backtest verdict, stated plainly** (307,875 stock-days since 2008, split at 2020): the tiers order **downside** correctly in both eras — 3M p10 pre-2020 runs −12.3% (Strong Buy) → −24.6% (Sell Immediately) — but **medians are flat across tiers** and Sell even bounces hardest. It manages risk; it does not pick winners. Fundamentals rules cannot be replayed (history began 2026-08-30) and the report says so.
- **All five advice columns are permanent since 2026-09-15 — the Rules picker is gone (owner's call).** Choosing which columns show is the job of personal column views (planned), not a picker per group; until then the Advice group collapses as a whole from the Columns menu. `applyAdviceCols()` survives only as the per-render hook for the filter summary, the `body.hide-av-*` rules and `extraProfiles` are deleted, and the screener no longer writes `prefs.advices` (the PUT whitelist still accepts it, harmlessly). *What follows describes the picker as it was:* **The advice is COLUMNS now, one per profile, and the Rules picker is a multi-select (owner, 2026-09-13).** The single Advice column is gone; in its place up to five advice columns inside the Advice group — **Balanced always first and always shown** (it appears in the menu as a locked always-on row), the other four toggled by the `Rules` picker (`n/5` trigger, menu stays open while toggling, choice saved in prefs as `advices`, an allowlisted id array). Each column carries the full kit: colours, the firing rule as tooltip, **its own change chevron against that profile's own yesterday**, its own sort (`av:<name>` sort keys), and **clicking a cell opens the ladder panel under that column's profile** (`panelProfile`; state cells open under Balanced, since the four state columns are pinned to Balanced). Columns hide/show by body class with the group banner's colspan recounted (`applyAdviceCols()` from `applyGroups()`) — headers are never rebuilt. Non-Balanced columns carry the teal tint (`td.avx`). `scoreActionRows()` runs ten engine passes (five profiles × today+yesterday, ~10ms) into `adviceBy`, stamping the Balanced results onto the classic `s.action*` fields for server parity — the CSV, hover card, emails and chatbot all still read Balanced and are untouched (the CSV's profile-suffix machinery was removed; the owner plans to retire the export). This absorbed and replaced the temporary TMP-5COLS inspection columns.
- **Clicking an Action cell opens the ladder panel** — the rule list as rungs (vetoes / caps / setups), the fired rung lit, rungs below it dimmed "not checked", every condition a mini-gauge (passing zone shaded, dot at the stock's value), establishment score as a 7-segment bar, and a model-level reference behind "How is this computed?" (three tabs, one per type). It is drawn from **`ActionRules.explain()` / `ladder()` in action.js, not from UI-side prose**: explain() emits the rungs from the same defs and rule order evaluate() walks and sets `mismatch` if the first all-conditions-met rung differs from evaluate()'s verdict — action-test.js sweeps 18,000 random row×profile evaluations asserting it never fires, and the live snapshot was checked row for row (93/93 agree). The panel recomputes client-side per click (`/action.js` is already loaded for severity sorting); no server changes. **Every other cell in the group opens a panel of its own** (`data-fam` on the cells → `openFamilyPanel`): Trend / Entry / Fundamentals draw that family's own priority ladder from `explain().families` — built by `buildFamilies()` in the same order `states()` collapses by, from the same `condKit()` builders the Action rungs use, with `familyMismatch` set if the lit rung ever differs from the column's word (in the same 18,000-evaluation sweep, and 93/93 live). Type shows the establishment points; Guards evaluates both checks with nothing dimmed, because guards stack rather than first-match.
- **The stock page carries a trend ribbon** — a strip under the chart painting the Trend column's word per session over the drawn range, replayed client-side through `ActionRules.trendAt()` (the engine's own `definitions()` + `states()` on a minimal row, exported for exactly this). Trend is the one family that is 100% bar-derived, so the ribbon is one uniform series over the whole archive with no fundamentals gap; breakdown thresholds follow the stock's **current** type, since the classification is not replayable — "today's rules, today's type, replayed", the momentum-recompute semantics. The averages come from the same `RowCard.sma` the MA overlays draw, so a colour change lands exactly where the lines cross; the crosshair readout gains a Trend row; `No data` stays unpainted. Verified: 400-random-readings property test (`trendAt` equals the column word), a 12-check headless replay over a synthetic rise-and-collapse, and today's word replayed from the archive against the scored snapshot — **92/92 exact**. **The dashed line trades ONE rule — the green states — and nothing else, at the owner's instruction (2026-09-13).** A Rules control briefly let the line trade a profile's verdict replay (`ActionRules.actionAt()`, tier ≥ BwR enters / Hold keeps / ≤ Avoid exits); the owner removed it after DELL made the case vivid — the verdict machine exited January's Breakdown and then sat flagged "Extended — wait for a pullback" for 96 straight sessions through a +250% recovery, a flat line under a solid green ribbon — and, decisively, **a full advice history is not honestly reconstructible, so the line may only claim what the bars can prove**. The verdict replay SURVIVES as an explainer only: the crosshair's Model row and the why card run it against Balanced. What the removed modes measured stays recorded: the five profiles replay nearly alike on technicals (their aggression is fundamentals-side, unreplayable); the real spread was trend-hold vs verdict replay (DELL +356% held vs +19.6% verdict-replayed). **"Why do these differ?"** in the ribbon legend opens `#whyBox` — `compareRuns()` diffs the two position series day by day, ranks the disagreement stretches by what the price did during each, quotes the model's own flag for every one, and `whyHtml()` templates the verdict sentence from the two measured buckets (entry friction vs held-through) — prose from numbers, never numbers from prose; the crosshair readout names the blocking flag on divergent days; the headline is test-pinned to equal both equity lines exactly. **The TREND toggle off sweeps everything trend**: line, ribbon, legend, why card (replay spreads measured while the modes existed: PTON −59% vs −5%, MU +11,485% vs +28,544%, NVDA the other way: +51,190% vs +15,775%). **Beside the price sits the trend-hold line** (TREND toggle, dashed because hypothetical): equity from holding only Strong uptrend / Above 200D, in cash otherwise, anchored to the price where the ribbon starts so flat shelves are the cash stretches and the gap between the lines is the verdict. **The word at day t earns the t→t+1 move** — the signal acts at the close it appears on, pinned by a headless test, the look-ahead class the strategy backtest caught once already. Pure price, zero costs, nothing earned in cash; the caption reports `Trend-held ±X% vs ±Y% hold · N round trips`, the trip count being the tell for what costs would take (30–80 trips on a Max view) — `/single` is the full instrument. Measured on the archive: PTON −59% vs −92% hold, ZM +140% vs +9%, KSS −70% vs −71%, NVDA +51,190% vs +28,522% — sidesteps the collapses, and at zero cost even keeps up with the monsters, which the trip count says not to over-read.
- **Days held — how long the Balanced verdict has stood (2026-09-17).** The owner's question: a Strong Buy that has held for weeks is a different proposition from one that turned this morning, and the chevron only says that it moved since the last close. `advice_state` (symbol, action, since_d, seen_d, sessions, exact) counts market days; `stampAdviceAge()` puts `adviceDays` / `adviceExact` on every row on the way out, the `stampShortNames` pattern, so a snapshot written before the field existed still carries it.
  - **It could not be backfilled, and the column says so.** Only ONE day of technicals is stored (`prevTech`, which is all `advicePrev` ever was), and a full verdict replay needs fundamentals — `fundamentals_history` begins 2026-08-30 and lands only on the days a Refresh all ran, **1,642 rows across 271 stocks, about six recorded days**. So the count starts when the column shipped and prints **≥N** until the row has actually been *seen* to change, after which it is exact. "At least" is the honest reading; zero would be a lie and a bare number would be a bigger one.
  - **Counted once a day, on the same gate `fundamentals_history` uses** — during a Refresh all, dated by `marketDay(rows)`. That run happens after the close, so the verdict being counted is the settled one. Counting on the intraday rounds would advance the clock against a provisional bar and reset the run every time a verdict flapped over lunch. `noteAdvice()` is idempotent per market day (a Refresh all is a dozen rounds, and may be re-run by hand) and a stale day can never rewind a run.
  - **A verdict that has moved since the last recorded close reads 1 session, exactly** — the stored run is yesterday's news, so the stamp overrides it rather than showing a count that has already ended.
  - Verified: 18 checks — the counter over simulated days (first sighting, ageing, the same day twice, a watched change restarting the run and making it exact, neighbours untouched, a stale day rewinding nothing), the stamp on real rows, and the column (beside Cushion, headers over cells, every banner spanning its own columns, the ≥ marker, alignment, sorting).
- **Cushion, and why the raw distance is not a safety ranking (2026-09-17).** The owner asked the right question — five stocks all read Strong Buy, which one — and the measurement answered it the other way round. `actionCushion` is `actionRisk.drop ÷ (realisedVol ÷ √12)`: the distance to the exit in the stock's **own monthly volatility**, stamped beside `actionRisk` in `scoreActionInto`. It is a column in the Advice group, it colours amber under 1σ and green at 2σ, and **sorting by any advice column breaks ties on it, thickest first whichever way the tier is sorted** — because it reads tail size, not direction. The research-log row above has the numbers; the short version is that raw room ranks the tail backwards and the σ figure ranks it correctly, and neither ranks return. Every surface says so: the column's tooltip, the ladder panel, the hover card's comment and Help §12. **"Risk to exit" was renamed "Distance to the exit"** everywhere for the same reason — it read as a safety score.
- **Risk to exit (2026-09-14)** — the honest half of the infographic's risk/reward: `exitDistance(r, cfg)` in action.js simulates a one-day decline in 0.5% steps over the ALL-TECHNICAL rule list (the same list the backtest replays) until the verdict reaches Avoid or worse, returning `{drop, action, flag}` or null past −60%. MAs, RSI, volume and fundamentals are held at today's values — a gauge of where the rules stand, not a stop order, and the panel says so. Server: `scoreActionInto` stamps `actionRisk` (Balanced) on every row on the way out; client: the ladder panel computes it under the OPEN profile via the same export. **drop 0 under a verdict still above Avoid is not a contradiction** — the gauge is technical-only, so it means fundamentals are doing the holding, and both surfaces word it exactly that way ("technicals at Avoid — fundamentals hold the verdict").
