**Decided with the owner before building**: the full all-technical verdict rather than the Trend word alone; a rolling sweep of many start dates rather than one window; a nightly table in Turso rather than committed JSON; and the bar backfill first.

- **Phase 0 — the backfill, done.** See the bar-archive section: 640,433 bars, the archive at **1.71M rows back to 2003**, and stocks reaching ten years up from 211 to **332**. Without it a nineteen-year sweep would silently have run on the deepest-held 39% of the universe, which is also its most survivor-biased slice.
- **Phase 1 — `techrow.js` and the `tech_history` table, done.**
  - **`techrow.js` exists because there were TWO row builders and this file claimed there was one.** `btRowAt` in server.js fed the advice backtest; `action-backtest.js` built its own inline. They had drifted in two ways that change verdicts: the 52-week high was the **intraday high** in one and the **closing high** in the other (measured 1.83 points apart on a test series, against a `near_high` threshold of −10), and `volTrend` was absent from the study's row entirely, so the `distribution` Avoid rule could never fire in eighteen years of it. Both now call the shared builder. Proven identical to the old server function by lifting it out of the previous commit and comparing 12,782 numbers across 900 sessions — worst gap **0**.
  - **`tech_history` stores the engine's INPUTS, not just its verdict** — `vs200`, `vs50`, `rsi`, `m1`, `m3`, `from_high`, `vol_trend`, `history_days` are every field the all-technical rules read, plus `close` so a sweep never touches `bars` at all. That makes the table **rule-set agnostic**: a page re-evaluates any preset at read time instead of being locked to whichever one the builder baked in. `action`/`flag` are kept as the Balanced reading for the fast path.
  - **Weekly marks, aligned across symbols.** The canonical mark is the last trading day of each week taken from the UNION of all dates, not per symbol — otherwise an equal-weight basket is not measured on one day. A symbol with no session within `STALE_DAYS` (8) of a mark is skipped rather than carried forward.
  - **It is REBUILDABLE**, unlike `fundamentals_history`: every field derives from bars. Delete the table and re-run `build-tech-history.js`.
  - **The nightly top-up is self-pacing** (`noteTechMark`): write a mark when the newest one is seven days old. No weekday arithmetic, no holiday list, and it heals after a missed week — where "write it on Fridays" would silently skip whenever a Friday was a holiday. It rides the Refresh-all gate beside `fundamentals_history` and `noteAdvice`, and costs nothing: every technical it stores was already computed for that row. **It stores the ALL-TECHNICAL verdict, deliberately not `row.action`** — mixing the two would make the column mean one thing before today and another after it.
  - **Multi-row inserts, and the measurement that forced them.** `writeTechHistory` first sent one statement per row in batches of 500, the shape every other writer here uses. Against production that measured **~1,000 rows a minute — five hours for the table.** The cost is per STATEMENT, not per row: 200 rows in one `values (...),(...)` runs at **23,525 rows a minute, a 23x speedup**, and the same 333k rows in about thirteen minutes. Worth remembering before any other bulk write here.
  - **The table is COMPLETE as of 2026-09-20: 332,883 rows, 421 symbols, 2004-01-14 → 2026-09-18.** The final resumed pass wrote 104,960 rows in 278 minutes.
  - **A dropped socket does not throw for HOURS, and the retry only fires on a throw.** That same run lost **159 minutes — 2h39m of its 4h38m — to one chunk**: the write at 60,000 stalled at the 72-minute mark and the `terminated` error did not surface until 231 minutes. `writeTechHistory` awaits `db.batch` with no deadline, so between the socket dying and libSQL noticing there is nothing to catch and nothing printed. From the outside it is indistinguishable from a hang: **the process stays alive, CPU sits near zero, stdout says nothing** — and that is exactly what it looks like when it is working normally too, because the job is network-bound. **So do not judge this build by CPU or by silence; judge it by the row count.** I called a healthy run dead on those signals and was wrong.
    - **Fixed the same day: `withDeadline(label, ms, run)` in db.js, and every `writeTechHistory` batch now runs under one.** It races the request against a clock, so silence becomes an ordinary throw — which `noteTechMark`'s caller already catches and logs, the rule that a history write never fails a refresh. `DB_BATCH_TIMEOUT_MS` (60s) is the default, chosen to suit a REQUEST path; the two real callers each say why they differ: **`noteTechMark` passes 30s** (optional work in the tail, and a missed mark self-heals because the writer is self-pacing) and **the offline builder passes 15 minutes**, because a *healthy* batch there takes 3-6 minutes against an archive this size. That still turns 159 minutes of silence into one retry.
      - **The request is NOT cancelled** — libSQL offers no handle for that — so this is only safe because every caller is an idempotent upsert. A caller that is not must not use it. The abandoned request settles minutes later and `withDeadline` attaches its own `catch`, or that late rejection arrives unhandled and takes the process down long after the code that started it gave up.
      - **`timer.unref()` was in the first cut and is a trap: never add it back.** An unref'd timer does not hold the event loop open, so in a CLI script — the builder, the one place this most has to work — a hung request leaves nothing else pending and **Node exits 0 mid-write instead of throwing**, turning the hang into a silent success. Caught by the test, which is itself a bare script; an assertion names the cause.
      - Verified: 13 checks, and the fixture is the point — **every hang is a promise that never settles**, not a rejection, because that is what actually happened. A stubbed throw would have proved nothing.
  - **`Buy with Risk` never appears** — `etfRules` has no path to it. The trend-only backtest has five usable tiers, not six.
  - **What the archive actually supports**: marks span 2004 → today, but 2004-2007 carry ONE symbol (the 5,000-bar API ceiling puts most deep symbols at 2006-11, plus 252 sessions of run-up). **The usable floor is 2008 with 278 symbols**, rising to 421 today — which does cover the financial crisis, the one stretch where a trend rule should prove itself.
  - Verified: the extraction (10 checks, identical to the last commit's function), the store round trip (18 checks — the round trip, replace-not-duplicate, windowing, purge, blanks staying null, and that **every rule set re-derives from a stored row**), the nightly mark through the REAL refresh path (11 checks — a Refresh all writes, a second the same day does not, a week later does, a plain Refresh never does, and the stored verdict re-derives), and the query plans against 344,000 rows (every read SEEKS; a one-year window is 22,360 rows in 60ms).
  - **Two fixture traps, both caught by an assertion passing on an empty set.** The refresh test stubbed the provider to refuse everything, so the refresh produced no rows and "every mark re-derives" passed over zero marks; it needs a price stub serving the fixture's own bars, **including the benchmark** — SPY is fetched on every refresh and is deliberately never archived, so returning an error for it failed the whole round. And the store test hand-wrote `action: 'Buy'` beside technicals that genuinely read Strong Buy; the fixture now derives the verdict the way the builder does.
- **Phase 2 — the sweep and the page, done.** `GET /api/trend-backtest` and **`/trend-backtest`** (admin, a row in the console's Research section beside the advice one).
  - **A FIXED forward horizon** (1M/3M/6M/1Y as 1/3/6/12 MONTHLY marks), never "carry to today" — that would give a 2008 window eighteen years and a 2026 one a fortnight, and averaging those compares nothing. *It was 4/13/26/52 weekly marks until 2026-09-20; see the read cost below for why whole months, and note the page had always said "held three months" while the code meant 91 days.*
  - **The benchmark is the equal-weight universe over the same window**, so the question is whether the rules SELECTED. Excess is basket − market, per window.
  - **A window with no picks is CASH, not a missing observation** — it returns 0 and its excess is minus the market. Dropping those would delete exactly the 2008-shaped windows a trend rule exists for, and would flatter every remaining one. **Thin windows (under 3 picks) are flagged, never filtered**: a filter there is a thumb on the scale.
  - **The two honesty numbers are computed and printed, not left to the reader.** The **effective sample** — monthly starts at a 3-month horizon overlap ~3:1, so ~220 windows is ~74 independent readings — is a CARD, not a footnote. And the **pool per window**, since it grows from 89 to 129 (and will grow further) across the span, so an early window is a narrower claim.
  - **`Buy with Risk` is shown struck through and disabled**: `etfRules` has no path to it, so the trend backtest has five usable tiers. Hiding it would read as an oversight.
  - **THE FIRST FULL-UNIVERSE READING (2026-09-20, the complete 332,883-row table — this is the number the module was built to produce).** 3M horizon, Strong Buy + Buy, Balanced, from 2008: **222 windows spanning 2008-01-02 → 2026-06-03, on a pool growing 268 → 417**, and **74 independent readings** after the 3x overlap discount.

| | median excess | hit rate | avg picks |
|---|---|---|---|
| all years (222 windows) | **−0.32%** | **44.6%** | 108 of 336 |
| pre-2020 (144) | −0.27% | 47.2% | 106 |
| post-2020 (78) | −0.89% | 39.7% | 112 |

    Mean excess −1.14%, p10/p90 −4.4/+2.7, worst −36.8%, best +6.7%. **The verdicts do not select: the basket loses to its own universe by a small margin, in both eras, and beats it less than half the time.** That is the research log's standing conclusion — the model orders downside and does not pick winners — now measured on twenty years rather than inferred from two months, and it agrees with the 18-year `action-backtest.js` re-measurement taken the same day on 1,588,936 stock-days.
    - **The likeliest reason is that this is barely a selection at all**: Strong Buy + Buy qualifies **108 of 336 stocks**, about a third of the universe, so the basket is close to being the benchmark with a third of the names dropped. A test with more discrimination (a top-N cut inside the tier, or Strong Buy alone) is the obvious next question and has not been run.
    - **The distribution is narrow and left-skewed** — p10 −4.4 against p90 +2.7, worst −36.8 against best +6.7. The big negative tail is the 2009 whipsaw the partial reading already showed, and it is what a trend rule does at a violent bottom, not a defect.
  - *(superseded by the reading above)* **First real reading (partial table, 89→129 symbols, 3M horizon, Strong Buy + Buy)**: median excess **−0.65%**, hit rate 42%, and the crisis behaves exactly as a trend rule should — picks fall 22 → 2 through the 2008 fall while beating equal weight by 3–15 points, then **1 pick through the March 2009 snap-back** for −28% and −40% excess. That whipsaw is also what the −132% worst window at 1Y is, and it is not a bug. Across all windows the rules slightly underperform, at every horizon, which is the research log's standing verdict now measurable from a page.
  - Verified: 31 checks on the sweep (basket, benchmark, excess, horizon and the overlap discount all against hand-computed values; a symbol with no end price excluded from BOTH legs; cash windows; theme scoping; the rule set re-evaluated from the stored inputs) and 31 on the page (both charts drawn and actually distributions, the table agreeing row-for-row with the API, sorting, the URL round trip, and **the honesty prose asserted as text** — a page that reports a median over overlapping windows without saying how few are independent is the exact failure this module exists to avoid).
  - **`#cards > .card`, not `#cards .card`** — the double-bezel puts the class on the shell AND the core, so the descendant selector counts every card twice. The `/quality` trap, met again in a test rather than in the page.
  - **The sweep read the WHOLE table, and that was three minutes (2026-09-20).** Measured against production: **181.8 seconds cold**, 0.4s warm, for a sweep whose arithmetic is **154ms** — so essentially all of it was moving 232,000 rows out of Turso, on a table still growing toward 333,000, against a documented response wall of about three minutes. The page was days from simply failing, and nothing on it would have said why. Three narrowings, in the order they mattered:
    - **The DATES.** The sweep starts on month-firsts, and with WHOLE-MONTH horizons it ends on one too — so one cached read of **273 marks out of 1,185** serves all four horizons. In weekly marks the end dates were arbitrary weeks, which meant reading the other three weeks of every month for the sake of them. This is the reason the horizon is months now; the statistics are if anything better, since "started in March 2012 and held three months" is what the page always claimed.
    - **The COLUMNS.** Balanced reads the stored verdict and the close and nothing else, so it asks for **four of fourteen**; a non-default rule set re-runs the engine and gets its own cached copy with the inputs. **The trap that buys is handing the slim row to a rule set that evaluates** — every input would be undefined, "No trend data" would fire on every row, and every window would quietly go to cash, which looks like a result. There is a test that runs a non-Balanced sweep with the slim copy warm in the cache and asserts it still finds picks.
  - **MEASURE ON AN IDLE DATABASE, or you will design around the wrong number.** Every figure in the bullet above was first taken while the `tech_history` bulk build was writing to the same database, and contention inflated them by 3-70x. Re-measured idle, at the table's full 332,883 rows:

| read | contended | idle |
|---|---|---|
| the whole table, 14 columns (the old path) | 181.8s *(at 232k rows)* | **126.2s / 106.7MB — and `terminated` on another run** |
| month-first marks, 4 columns (Balanced) | 64.6s | **5.7-7.7s** |
| month-first marks, 14 columns (other rule sets) | 166.8s | **23-25s** |
| `select distinct d` for the mark calendar | 429.9s | **~250ms** |

    **The old path is MARGINAL rather than merely slow, and that is the real finding.** Two idle runs minutes apart: one carried all 332,883 rows in 126.2s, the next answered `terminated`. 106.7MB is past the 83MB at which this file's own wall table records a failure, so the page worked or failed by luck on every cold instance — the hardest kind of fault to diagnose, because reproducing it is a coin toss. The narrowing takes the Balanced path to **5.7-7.7s and 13.4MB**, roughly **17-22x**, and puts it nowhere near the wall. **A third change was built on the bad number and then reverted the same day**: a `tech_marks` table holding nothing but the ~1,200 mark dates, justified by that 429.9s. Idle, the query it replaced is ~250ms against the table's ~100ms — 150ms inside a six-second operation, which does not buy a second copy of derivable state plus a write to keep in step. Production still holds the now-unused table; it is harmless and empty of consequence. The lesson is the measurement discipline, not the table.
  - **`readTechMarks` (a whole range, every column) is kept for offline work and nothing on a request path may call it.** It is the shape that cost the 181.8 seconds.
- *(superseded)* **Phase 2 — the sweep and the page: not built yet.** Planned: every month-start across the archive with a FIXED forward horizon (1M/3M/6M/12M) rather than "carry to today", measured benchmark-relative against the universe's own equal-weight return, reporting the distribution — median excess, hit rate, p10, worst window, and the pre/post-2020 split. Two numbers it must print or it misleads: the **effective sample** (monthly starts at a 3-month horizon overlap ~3:1, so ~230 windows is ~75 independent observations — the research log's own discount rule) and the **symbol count per window**, since the pool shrinks going back.

### The Size card — companies as concentric circles
**A tenth template (2026-09-20, owner's request): compare companies within a theme by size, carry other fundamentals with it, and do it as circles — "maybe two concentric circles, each representing a different measure".**

- **AREA is the encoding, never radius.** A circle of twice the radius is four times the area, so sizing by radius would quadruple the apparent gap — and in the direction people already misread a bubble chart. `r = R * sqrt(v / vmax)`, pinned by a test against arithmetic rather than by eye, and the card says so in as many words because the misreading is the default.
- **Both discs share ONE area scale whenever both are money**, which is what makes the pair worth drawing rather than decorative: the second disc's share of the first IS the ratio between the two figures. Revenue inside market cap is price-to-sales drawn rather than stated. **When revenue EXCEEDS the cap the discs simply swap** — the company trading under one times sales is the one whose circle breaks out of the ring, which is exactly what an eye should catch. Clipping it would have hidden the most interesting case.
- **A RATIO has no scale of its own**, so a margin is drawn as a share of the outer area: 50% fills half the disc. Clamped at the edge, since a margin over 100% is a data fault rather than a bigger circle, and a negative one draws no core at all.
- **The two guards are the fundamentals ranking's own, and they matter more here.** Every absolute figure is in the company's OWN reporting currency, so a single foreign reporter would dominate the picture for the wrong reason (Samsung's revenue is in won) — non-USD rows are dropped from any money comparison. And gross margin and net cash mean nothing for a lender, so banks are dropped from those.
- **A THIRD measure rides as plain text under each circle** (2026-09-20, owner's request — "a third metric like P/E or Forward P/E, only as text, just to show the valuation difference"): forward or trailing P/E, P/S, P/B, EV/EBITDA, PEG or market cap. **Deliberately not a third ring** — the card already encodes two things by area, and a multiple is a number you read one company at a time rather than a magnitude you compare at a glance; three nested circles would be decoration pretending to be data. Quieter than the two the circles carry, in the mono face, and **named once in the legend and once in the caption rather than beside every circle**, since twelve repetitions of "fwd P/E" is noise on a poster.
  - **Every multiple is the PROVIDER's own ratio, never one divided here.** Computing price-to-sales from a dollar market cap and a won revenue would produce a confident, meaningless number — the ADR trap this file records twice already.
  - **A multiple off a loss is arithmetic, not cheapness**, so a P/E at or below zero is a dash, never a negative number that would read as cheap. The same rule the Quality score and the fundamentals ranking apply.
  - **A dash means TWO different things and the caption separates them**: "1 had no meaningful multiple: a ratio off a loss is arithmetic, not cheapness. 1 does not report it." Drawing both the same and then counting one as the other is how a card quietly lies — the News column's and the data-quality page's lesson, met a third time.
- **A circle under 14px is floored AND MARKED** with a `·`, never silently enlarged: a dot drawn at its honest size is still the truth.
- **The cell is sized from the artboard's OWN height**, not the column count. A card cannot scroll — a grid too tall does not clip, it sits on top of the masthead, because `.s-body` centres its content. The first cut fixed the cell per column count and overflowed the SQUARE by 93px at four circles, the shortest artboard with the largest cells. The test sweeps 3 shapes × 5 counts × 3 inner measures and measures the artboard.
- **IT OVERFLOWED ANYWAY, and the sweep said it did not (2026-09-20).** Reported from the studio: the kicker printed on top of the brand tagline. Three things had to be got right to see it at all:
  - **`.s-in` is a fixed-height flex column, so its children overflow WITHOUT growing it.** Reading its `scrollHeight` answers "fits" while the body is spilling over the header. The honest numbers are **`.s-body`'s own overflow** and the gap between the kicker's box and the tagline's.
  - **It reproduced only in the STUDIO.** A standalone harness rendering the same card measured +4px of clearance and never collided; the studio measured −9px. Sweeping the card off-page is not the same as sweeping it where it is used, and the bug was reported against the studio.
  - **It was NOT the third measure**, which is what I assumed and said out loud — it overlapped with that switched off too. `FURNITURE` was simply set below what the card takes: measured, everything above the grid is ~395px and below it ~197px, against a reserve of 530.
  - **A single constant is a guess at variable content, which is why it now carries headroom rather than sitting on the measurement.** The caption GROWS — a sentence when circles are floored, one for a multiple off a loss, one for a figure not reported — so the tightest case is a card with both a loss-maker and an unreported figure in it, and a fixture of clean data never finds it. **`MAXR` also reserves the text height inside the cell now** (two name lines, the value, and the third measure when shown), so the disc shrinks to make room rather than the text overflowing the cell it sits in.
  - Verified by `size-fit-test.js`: **135 combinations in the studio** — 3 shapes × 5 counts × 3 third measures × 3 inner measures — asserting the kicker never reaches the tagline AND that the body never overflows at all, since a body that spills a pixel today collides on tomorrow's data. Tightest clearance 13px, zero overflowing.
- **NO BACKTICKS IN `STYLE`.** It is itself a template literal, so one inside a CSS comment ends the string and the whole module stops parsing — `ReferenceError: sub is not defined`, nowhere near the cause. Cost one debugging round.
- **A screenshot of a card is worthless without `app.css`.** The test page injected `Cards.STYLE` alone, so every token (`--green`, `--text`, the faces) was undefined, the inner disc rendered invisible and the picture said nothing about the design. The overflow measurements were unaffected; the eye check was not.
- Verified: 23 checks on the card (the area encoding against arithmetic, the shared scale, the swap, a margin filling its share, both guards, the floor marker, the words, every artboard × count × measure for overflow, and that the two discs are genuinely CONCENTRIC — same centre, whatever their sizes) and 13 on the studio wiring (the picker, the box, each control reaching the card, the industry nesting, and a **saved post restoring the template and its controls**, since a post stores controls rather than a picture).

### The Bubble card — two measures against each other, area as a third
**An eleventh template (2026-09-20, owner's request): "show the chart as bubble instead of concentric circles, or add a new template called Bubble".** Built as a new template rather than an option on Size, for a reason worth keeping:

- **A single circle per company ALREADY EXISTS** — Size with the inner measure set to none — so the option reading was very nearly a no-op. The reading worth building is the other one: a chart where **position** carries meaning. Size ranks magnitudes in a grid; Bubble asks how two measures relate and lets a third set the weight of each answer.
- **It is also not the quadrant card** (`fund` mode `quad`), which plots one FIXED pair — revenue growth against profit margin — with every dot the same size. Here both axes and the area are chosen, which makes it a different question rather than a restyling.
- **The measures are `FUND_METRICS`, exported as `Cards.bubbleMeasures()`** and used to build the studio's three pickers. Seventeen measures, one list: a copy in the markup would drift the first time one was added, and a test asserts the picker matches the catalogue exactly.
- **`plotScales` was extracted from `scatterSvg`** so the axis arithmetic — the middle-range span, the padding, the stray pinning — is not written twice. The quadrant card's own output is asserted unchanged afterwards, because a refactor that quietly rewrites a published chart is the failure to fear.
- **THE PERCENTILE TRIM DOES NOTHING AT SMALL n, which is why `fence: 'iqr'` exists.** The 2%/98% trim drops three dots at each end of the quadrant card's ~140 and behaves; at the 20 a Bubble shows, `floor(20 * 0.02)` is 0 and it trims **nothing** — so one company at 900% growth sets the axis, presses every other circle into a band a few pixels tall, and nothing is ever marked as pinned. Tukey's fence (a quartile either side, 1.5x the spread) is robust at any size and is what a small plot asks for.
- **A CIRCLE IS NOT A DOT, and two bugs came from forgetting it — both found by a screenshot, neither by an assertion.** Pinning a stray puts its CENTRE on the axis edge, which hung half of NVIDIA off the artboard: the biggest company on the card, sliced in two, *because* it was the furthest out. Centres are kept a radius inside the plot now. And labels anchored left or right of the mark — fine beside the quadrant card's 7px dots — printed "JPMorgan Chase" straight across Microsoft at 66px. A name sits **inside** its circle when the circle can hold it (the one thing a bubble can do that a scatter cannot), otherwise centred **below**; and a label that would land on one already placed is **dropped**, because an unreadable name is noise and hides the mark underneath.
- **AREA is the encoding, never radius**, checked against arithmetic rather than by eye: caps of 400/100/25 must give radii 1 : 0.5 : 0.25, where sizing by radius would give 1 : 0.25 : 0.0625. The card says so on its own face, because the misreading is the default.
- **The fundamentals guards travel with it**, and matter more here than on a ranking, because an axis is a comparison by construction: non-USD reporters are dropped from any money comparison (Samsung's revenue is in won), banks from gross margin and net cash, and loss-makers from a P/E or PEG axis. A company with **no positive area measure is drawn as a ring** at the floor size and counted in words — it still has a place on both axes, and either inventing an area or dropping it silently would misreport the screen.
- **A FOURTH measure rides as text, the Size card's own** (2026-09-20, owner: "also show as text, what you have done in the size chart"). Same catalogue (`SIZE_THIRDS`) and the same rules, now in one shared `thirdReader()` that both cards call — the provider's own ratio rather than one divided here, a **dash for a multiple off a loss** (arithmetic, not cheapness), and the **two kinds of blank counted separately**, because a company that loses money and one that does not report the figure are different facts and drawing them the same is how a card quietly lies.
  - **It rides the NAME, not every circle.** Twenty positioned, overlapping circles cannot each carry two lines of text without exactly the collisions the labelling rules exist to prevent. So it appears on the companies that are labelled — and the caption says **"Of the companies named, …"**, since a count over a subset must not be worded as a claim about the screen.
- Verified: 33 checks off-page (the area arithmetic, the guards, rings, pinning, each channel following its picker, an unknown measure key falling back, three artboards, the third measure's dash for a loss counted apart from an absent figure, money formatted currency-neutral, and the quadrant card unchanged) and 23 in the real studio, plus screenshots at true 1080px — which is what caught both drawing bugs.
  - **Two fixture traps worth keeping**: the loss-maker has to be big enough to be NAMED or its dash is never drawn and the count stays at zero; and `forwardPe: 8 + (cap % 40)` gave every company `8.0×`, a screenshot that showed the feature working and demonstrated nothing.

#### The saved-post option cap was silently eating controls
**`cleanPosts` capped a post at 40 option keys while the studio collected 63, so everything past the fortieth was dropped on save.** Found while testing Bubble's round trip; it had been true for every card whose controls sit late in `CONTROL_IDS` — **all seven of Size's, both of Intro's, and `annKick`/`annHead`/`annBody`, the Announcement card's free text, lost without a word.**

- The cap is a guard against free storage, not a budget, and **it must clear `CONTROL_IDS` with room to spare** — a template is added in promo.html and this number is nowhere near it. It is `POST_OPT_MAX` (100) now, against 63.
- **The test passed throughout, and that is the lesson.** `size-studio-test.js` saved a post, switched template, reopened it and asserted `sizeOuter === 'rev'` — **without ever moving `sizeOuter` away**, so it read a value that had never left the page. **A round-trip test that does not perturb the thing it round-trips tests nothing.** Both harnesses move every control now before reopening.

## The pivot view
**`/pivot` — the screen counted by two dimensions at once (2026-09-20, owner's request), a button in the screener bar beside Promo Studio.** Member page, guests refused like the studio: a preview of five stocks has no shape to show. Rows and columns are chosen from Sector, Industry, Theme, Balanced advice, Trend state, Entry state, Company type and Exchange; every cell is a count, and clicking one expands the stocks in it underneath the row.

- **No endpoint, no query, nothing on the rows-read meter.** Every dimension is already on a snapshot row, so the page reads `/api/stocks` once and cross-tabulates in the browser — measured 2.1MB and 11.6s cold, then every change of dimension is instant. Exploration means changing the axes constantly, which is exactly when a round trip per change is the wrong shape.
- **THE CARDINALITIES WERE MEASURED BEFORE IT WAS DESIGNED**, and they decided the design: sector 11, industry **87**, advice 6, trend 6, type 3, exchange 4, theme 26 — over 430 stocks. Sector × advice is 66 cells at ~6.5 stocks each and reads well; **industry × anything is 522 cells for 430 stocks and is mostly empty**, with the average industry holding five companies. So a dimension past `WIDE_CAP` (24) values **folds its tail into one "Other" bucket**, and the test proves the fold loses nobody — the totals still reconcile.
- **Reconciliation is the assertion that matters.** A pivot whose totals do not add up is worse than no pivot: it looks authoritative and is wrong. Every cell is summed against its row total, the row totals against the grand total, and the grand total against the stock count.
- **A THEME IS MULTI-VALUED, and that breaks the arithmetic everyone expects.** 33 stocks sit in more than one theme and **189 in none**, so with Theme on an axis the cells add up to more than the universe. The page says so in words — "the cells add up to 9, not 8 — 1 stock is counted in more than one bucket" — rather than printing a total that quietly disagrees with itself. `multi` is a property of the dimension, not a special case buried in the sum.
- **A blank is a BUCKET, never a dropped row.** 20 stocks have no sector yet, because profiles arrive on rotation; omitting them would make every total wrong while looking tidy. Shown as `—`, sorted last, and counted in the note.
- **No money measures, deliberately.** Summing revenue would add SEK to USD: `currency` reads USD for all 430 because it is the TRADING currency, while revenue and net income are in the company's **reporting** currency, which is not stored. Counts cannot lie that way. Market cap would be safe and is left for when it is missed.
- **A ladder is not the alphabet.** Advice, trend, entry and type order by their own ladders; everything else orders by size, because "where is the weight" is what a pivot is opened to answer.
- **THE SNAPSHOT'S `action` IS IGNORED ON READ** — `scoreActionInto` re-derives the verdict, company type, trend and entry from each row's technicals every time. A fixture that writes `action: 'Strong Buy'` therefore tests nothing: the first version of this test did exactly that, got one verdict for all eight stocks, and its ladder assertion passed on a **single column**. The fixture carries real technicals now, checked against `Action.apply` beforehand to confirm they produce five different verdicts.
- Verified: 30 checks on the real page — reconciliation in both the single- and multi-valued cases, the blank bucket, the ladder order, drill-down (expanding, the count matching the list, each row linking to its stock page, closing again), swap, the same-dimension-twice guard, the wide fold keeping every stock, and no sideways scroll at 390px.

## The advice backtest
**`/backtest` (admin, a row in the console's Research section) — re-run the rules on a past date, buy what they picked, carry it to today (2026-09-17, owner's request).** The owner's framing: "which stocks were a Strong Buy a month ago, equal-weight them, compare against an index". A real page rather than the throwaway script this project's research has lived in, because the owner intends to use it as the universe grows toward 1,000.

- **Two months is the hard cap**, enforced in the UI and again in the route. Past that the share of the verdict that is imputed grows without bound; the refusal says so rather than just failing.
- **The trade log — what was sold and bought, when, and why (2026-09-19, owner's request).** A rebalance reported only as "110.7% turnover" is a number nobody can check. `btSimulate` now names the symbols at the same diff that measures turnover — so the two can never tell different stories — and the page lists them under the chart as one collapsible block per rebalance (date · sold N · bought N · turnover · names held), the first open.
  - **The reason beside each name is the verdict that fired for it on that date**, which for a sale is precisely the one that disqualified it. So the route keeps **every** verdict at each mark, not only the qualifying ones — the target `Set` alone cannot explain a sale.
  - **A sale also reports what the position did while it was held** (entry date, sessions, close-to-close return), from an `entry` map kept alongside `held`. Sales list worst-first and purchases best-verdict-first, so the per-side cap (`BT_TRADE_CAP`, 40) trims the least interesting end; a trimmed list says how many it left out.
  - **In a re-run, a name that is KEPT is re-weighted rather than traded** — that re-weighting is part of the turnover figure but is neither a purchase nor a sale, and the note on the page says so, or the listed names will not add up to the percentage above them.
  - **It immediately found a real defect, which is the argument for building it.** A Top-N cut was applied at the open and **never again**: "top 5, rebalanced weekly" quietly held all 20 from the first mark, and nothing on the page could have shown it. The cut is re-applied at every re-run now, through `btPick` with the same rank — **exit-only is deliberately exempt**, since selling a name for ranking low is not what "sell on downgrade" means. The random control's seed moves with the mark, so it draws a fresh basket each period.
  - **`btRankMetrics(row, rows, i)` was extracted for it** — cushion and momentum as of one session, shared by the opening pick and every rebalance, so the cut cannot rank on a different measure from the one the run was set up with. It is computed only for names that qualify, and only when a cut has to be made: `Momentum.scoreBars` is a pass over the bars, and running it for the whole universe at every mark would be the expensive half of the run for a number nothing reads.
  - **A sale under a cut can happen with the verdict INTACT** — the name was ranked out, not downgraded. Without marking that (`ranked` on the trade), the log reads as selling a Hold to buy a Hold; it says `Still Hold — ranked below the top 5 by thickest cushion` instead.
  - Verified: 60 checks against the real server on an in-memory database plus the real page — chiefly **reconciliation**, replaying the listed trades forward from the opening basket (`p.selected`, not the return-sorted head of `picks`) and landing on the simulation's own book at every mark and at the end; every reported return recomputed straight from the bars; session counts measured on the trading axis; exit-only never buying and its sales all at or below the exit tier; the cut holding at 5 across every mark; and on the page, one block per rebalance, the rows, the rank-out wording, the buy rows carrying no return, and no sideways scroll at 390px. **The page assertions deliberately open the first block that actually TRADED** — the first rebalance was quiet, and "a row for every trade" passed on `0 === 0` until that was fixed, the same shape that has caught this project before.
- **It runs under any of the five rule sets, not just Balanced (2026-09-19, owner's request — "the other Advice such as Trend Rider and Max Risk").** A `Rules` picker beside Verdicts; `?rules=` on the route, validated against **`Cards.ADV_PROFILES`** (`RULE_SETS` in server.js) so the list is the studio's and is never restated — Conservative stays out of both. `ruleCfg(name)` resolves and caches each config, since `Action.resolve` clones DEFAULTS on every call. This is the one place a second rule set answers a question rather than showing a different word: the screener's columns and the studio's cards stay Balanced by design.
  - **The config reaches everything, not just the verdict**: `btEvalAt` at the start date AND at every rebalance, `btRankMetrics`' cushion, and **`actionNow`** — the snapshot's stamped verdict is Balanced's, so under any other rule set "verdict now" is re-evaluated against the live row, or the two columns compare two different machines.
  - **Every rule set is priced on the same run** (`compare` on the response, drawn as the clickable strip under the chart). The row build — bars, the 52-week window, a Wilder RSI — is rule-set independent and is the expensive half, so five rule sets cost four more `evaluate` calls on a row already in hand. **Buy-and-hold only**, because for equal dollars held a basket's return IS the mean of its members' returns; rebalancing five of them would be five more simulations, and the note says so rather than letting the strip look like it carries the run's own rebalancing. Cross-checked in the tests against a real run under each rule set — same picks, same return to 0.01pt.
  - **Two things that look like seams are not, and both cost a wrong assertion first.** **`chase` (max_1m / max_vs50 / max_rsi — the whole of Trend Rider's diff for an Established company) can never move a tier**: `cleanEntry` needs vs50 within −3..+8 and RSI within 45..65, strictly inside every chase threshold, so a stock separated by one of them has already failed cleanEntry and lands on "No clean entry" under both. Loosening chase changes the FLAG, not the verdict. And **cushion is identical across rule sets**: `exitDistance` walks the all-technical list, whose first Avoid is `downtrend` (below the 200-day AND under the 50-day), and neither reads a threshold any preset changes — the `stop_loss` rules that do vary return Sell Immediately, past the tier the walk stops at. Passing the config into `btRankMetrics` is correctness plumbing for the day a preset moves `exit.*`, not a behaviour change today.
  - **Where the rule sets really differ, measured against the engine rather than read off the presets**: `entry.buy_max_drawdown` (Balanced −20, Max Risk −30, Dip Buyer −35 — a deep-drawdown clean entry is Buy with Risk under one and a plain Buy under the other), `trend_gate` (Dip Buyer alone buys below the 200-day), `early.allow_strong_buy` (Trend Rider and Max Risk make an Early company a Strong Buy), and `stop_loss` (Aggressive −15 / Max Risk −20 call Avoid where Balanced calls Sell Immediately).
  - **A third trap for fixtures**: a row whose fundamentals are neither strong, ok nor weak falls through to **"No clean entry"**, which reads as a technical refusal and is not one — `fund_early.strong` needs revenue growth, gross margin **and positive FCF**.
  - Verified: 43 checks. The fixture is the test — five cohorts, one per seam above, each five stocks, and **the five rule sets come out with five DIFFERENT signatures**, so a run that ignored the parameter would fail four of the five rows. RSI is *pinned*, not sampled: Wilder's RS over a repeating 7-day cycle of five gains of g and two losses of l converges to 5g/2l whatever the phase, and sampling it from noise made neighbouring candidates disagree at random. A 24% fall is spread over ten sessions because **Wilder's average keeps a one-day gap alive for ~40 sessions** and holds RSI out of the cleanEntry window long after it. Plus the comparison cross-check, the rebalance following the rules, validation, and the page.
  - **`.mk` was already taken on this page** — the amber "reported" badge, defined unscoped and carrying a border — so a new empty `<span class="mk">` drew a 56px bordered pill beside every row. A screenshot found it; no assertion would have. **Fourth unscoped class on this page to capture later markup** after `.warn`, `.card` and `input[type="text"]`. It is `.tag`, scoped, with an `:empty` rule and a test asserting an empty marker takes no width.
- **What is replayed and what is invented, which is the whole honesty of the page and is printed ON it:** trend, entry, RSI, volume, the 50/200-day averages and the 52-week position are rebuilt from archived bars at that date — `btRowAt()` is `action-backtest.js`'s row builder, the same fields and the same arithmetic, so the page and the 18-year study cannot drift. RSI comes from `Momentum.rsiSeriesAt`, the app's own Wilder series. **Every fundamental is today's**, and so is the company type that follows from it, because `fundamentals_history` begins 2026-08-30. The verdict is `Action.evaluate(row, ACTION_CFG)` — the same resolved Balanced profile every other surface scores against.
- **The next earnings date is read from the archive as of that date**, not borrowed from today, so the "earnings soon" guard sees what it would have seen. `readEarningsDates()` over a bounded range; `earnings_history` gained an index on `d` for it, since the primary key is `(symbol, d)` and cannot seek on a date alone. **`earnings_history.d` IS the report date** (verified: AAPL 2026-07-30) and **`reported` is TEXT** ("After Hours"), not a flag — a `reported = 1` filter silently matches nothing.
- **Fundamentals are READ from the archive where they exist (2026-09-18).** `readFundamentalsAsOf(date)` returns, per symbol, the newest `fundamentals_history` row on or before the start date — genuinely point-in-time, because a row exists only for days a Refresh all ran and these values move in steps at earnings, so the last recorded set before a date is what stood on it. **The table's 42 fields cover every field the Balanced gate and `classify()` read**; the one it does not store is `qualityRating`, and Balanced has `use_quality: false`, so it never reads it. Today's values are the fallback, not the default, and the page reports the split as a card and a sentence. Measured on introduction: a 2026-09-14 start had 267 of 347 stocks on recorded data, 2026-09-01 had 70, and 2026-08-19 had none — the coverage gradient is the whole story, and it improves by a day per Refresh all.
  - **Ordered by `d` ALONE, not `(symbol, d)`.** Ordering by the primary key makes SQLite walk it as a covering index and ignore the date filter — a scan of the whole table, which `query-plan-test.js` caught immediately. Ascending by `d`, the last row seen per symbol is still its newest.
  - Proved by seeding a WEAK recorded set for a stock whose current fundamentals are strong: its verdict at that date drops from `Strong Buy` to `Hold (Weak fundamentals)`. Asserting on the counts alone would not have shown which set the engine actually read.
- **A coverage strip beside the date picker answers "what will I get" BEFORE a run (2026-09-18).** One bar per day the page will accept, its height the share of the universe whose fundamentals were recorded by then; clicking a bar picks that date, so it is a control rather than a decoration. **Cumulative, not the per-day count** — a date gets every symbol recorded on or before it, because fundamentals are step functions. `GET /api/backtest/coverage` (admin, cached 5 min) derives it from `readFundamentalsFirstSeen(since)`.
  - **Two indexed range reads, no `group by`.** `group by symbol` makes SQLite walk the primary key as a covering index and ignore the date filter — fine over 2,000 rows, ~365,000 a year at 1,000 stocks, and `query-plan-test.js` refused it twice before this shape passed. Who was recorded before the window (they count for every date in it) plus the rows inside it ordered by `d`, first occurrence wins.
  - A proposal for a whole coverage PAGE was declined: the spread is 1-15 days today only because 83 stocks joined that afternoon and two runs were abandoned, and every completed run adds a day to every stock at once, so the variation converges to noise. Per-stock coverage is the symptom; run completion is the cause, and `/refreshes` already shows that. The strip earns its place by sitting where the choice is made.
- **The look-ahead is measured rather than argued about.** Fundamentals move in steps at earnings, so imputing them is near-harmless for a company that did not report inside the window and a genuine look-ahead for one that did. The page counts the picks that reported and marks them in the table. Measured on the real universe: a 30-day window had **3** contaminated picks, a 60-day window had **49** — which is the single best argument for the two-month cap, and for reading the shorter window first.
- **Smaller batches of the same question: one bar read, cached 10 minutes, spanning `BT_WINDOW_DAYS` (530) so it covers every date the page can ask for.** Changing the date or the tiers re-runs against bars already in memory — zero extra database reads. At 1,000 stocks that read is ~650k rows and must not happen per click. Measured at 354 symbols: ~1s a run after the first.
- **Three curves on one shared scale**: the equal-weight basket, **the universe's own equal-weight return**, and SPY. The universe line is the one that answers *did the verdicts select* — the S&P is the flattering comparison, because a universe of high-beta names beats the index in most months whatever the screen does. Equal weight, never rebalanced, no costs, no dividends, all stated on the page.
- **SPY is fetched, not archived** (the benchmark is deliberately never stored), cached 30 minutes, 1 credit when cold. A benchmark failure degrades to two curves and a note rather than failing the run. **`BENCHMARK` moved to module scope for this** — it was declared inside `computeStocks`, so the first cut's SPY loader threw a ReferenceError that the try/catch turned into a quiet "benchmark unavailable".
- **The owner's calls, against my advice in two places and recorded as theirs:** recently-added stocks are INCLUDED (the page says so, and says what it does to a result); one start date at a time rather than a rolling sweep; multi-select tiers.
- **The page states its own limits in a panel that is not optional** — what is replayed, what is imputed, the contaminated-pick count, that recent additions are included, that one window is one observation that cannot separate rules from luck, and that the S&P flatters. Six of the 29 checks assert that prose is present, because it is the part most likely to be quietly dropped in a later edit.
- Verified: 29 checks against the real server on an in-memory database with the provider stubbed — validation (missing / malformed / beyond two months / future / unknown verdict), the arithmetic (a single pick's basket equals its own return, the return equals the close-to-close move, the start price is that session's close), the universe curve holding every stock and differing from the basket, tier selection changing the basket, a failed benchmark still returning a result, and the page (cards, three lines, chips from the query, the two-month cap on the picker, no sideways scroll, the explanation text, no console errors). Two fixture traps worth knowing: **a monotonic price series pegs RSI at 100** and fires "Extended", so a test series needs a wobble; and the engine reads **`revenueGrowthYoY` / `earningsGrowthYoY`**, not the shorter names — getting that wrong reports "fundamentals not OK" for a company with excellent numbers.

## Screens (the Yahoo-style screener)
**`/analysis` was folded into a Screens menu on the screener on 2026-09-15** (owner's decisions: picker beside View, starter screens only — no personal screens —, the full Yahoo-mapped table plus the seven /analysis screens, two new data fields, /analysis retired, no market-wide movers). `/analysis` now redirects to `/`; `analysis.html` is deleted. **Members lost the /analysis weighting selector with it** — the momentum lens is now set only from /admin, for the account doing the setting.

- **A screen is `{filters, sector?, industry?, advice?, changed?, sort, columns}`** — filter-row grammar for the filters, the bar pickers' state for the bound ones, and a column list applied like a view. Stored in `screens` (id, name, grp, position, description, def JSON), **seeded once** from `STARTER_SCREENS` in server.js (22 screens in six groups) behind the `app_meta` marker `screens_seeded`. `GET /api/screens` (any signed-in, guests included), `PUT /api/screens` (admin, whole-list replace through `cleanScreens()`: keys and columns shape-checked, values capped, sort dir forced to ±1).
- **Applying one** (`applyScreen`) saves the table's state the first time (`screenBase`: sort, filters, bound pickers, Changed, filter-row visibility), sets the screen's, opens the filter row when it shows anything, and **overrides the view through `activeView()`** — `activeViewId` and the saved pref are never touched. **Clear screen** restores `screenBase`. Picking a View while a screen is active keeps the filters and drops the columns (`screenColsOff`); **any difference from the definition reads "(edited)"** (`screenEdited`). Nothing is persisted — filters are not saved, and a screen is a set of filters.
- **Live counts** per screen in the menu, over `tabStocks()` (the portfolio in view), computed by `screenRows()` with the same `compileFilter` / `filterValue` the table uses.
- **Filter extensions the screens needed**: list filters accept either-of values (`Breakdown|Downtrend`, shown "Breakdown or Downtrend"); **screen-only keys** with no column — `fresh3mHigh` (Yes/No), `lastSurprise`, `daysSinceEarnings` — labelled by `SCREEN_ONLY_KEYS` in the filter strip.
- **Owner editing is from the table**: set it up, then **Save the table as a starter screen** (name, heading, one line), or the refresh icon beside a screen to replace it with the table as it is now, or delete. Everyone else sees a lock on the heading.
- **A pencil beside each screen edits it directly (2026-09-17).** The owner's note: there was no way to change which fields a screen shows — you had to apply it, rearrange the table and press Replace, which overwrote the filters as well. The pencil edits **name, heading, one line and columns**, and deliberately **leaves the filters and the sort alone**; those are edited on the table where you can watch them work, and captured with Replace. The dialog says so.
  - **It reuses `#viewDialog` rather than growing a third column picker.** `viewEdit.mode` is `'view'` or `'screen'`; a screen shows `#vdMeta` (heading and one line) and a Save screen button, a view hides it. `paintViewList()` is untouched — the tile dialog has its own painter only because it lists rowcard FIELDS rather than table columns, which is genuinely different data.
  - **Columns are stored in the table's order, not the order they were ticked in**, matching how a view behaves, and the save carries `def.filters`, `def.sort` and the bound pickers through untouched. Editing the screen that is currently applied redraws the table under the new columns.
  - **A screen's id must be exactly eight lowercase alphanumerics** — `cleanScreens()` checks it with `VIEW_ID_RE` and silently issues a random one otherwise. That is why `STARTER_SCREENS` reads `rec52hi0` and `daygainr` rather than `hi52w` and `gainers`: every id was padded to eight. A test fixture using a seven-character id had its screen re-idded mid-run, which is exactly what would happen to a hand-added starter screen.
  - Verified: 14 checks — the three tools on each row, the editor opening with the screen's name, heading, line and columns already ticked, adding and removing a field, the stored result, **the filters and sort unchanged**, the applied screen redrawing with the new columns, and the view editor still opening as a view.
- **Six new columns**, all derived from bars already fetched (no credits) and filled from the next refresh: **Relative** gains `52W Lo days` / `52W Hi days` (`daysSince52wLow/High`, sessions since the extreme — `extremeAges()`) and `52W range` (`range52Pos`, already computed, never shown before); **Volume** gains `Rel vol` (`volX`), `Volume` and `$ Volume` (`volume`, `dollarVolume` = volume × close, trading currency). Row width is 64 cells (`PAD_SPAN`), the error row spans 59, the empty row 61.
- **Honest labels kept from the discussion**: Bullish/Bearish are our Trend/Entry rules, Strong Buys is the Balanced rule set, earnings growth is last quarter YoY (Yahoo's growth screens use forecasts, Ultra-plan only), PEG is unreliable for loss-makers.
- Verified: headlessly as member (34) and owner (36) against a stub — the 64-cell rows and aligned headers, 22 screens with counts matching independent counts, a screen's rows / sort / columns / filter-row inputs / chips, "edited", sector-bound, either-of, screen-only chip, Advice and Changed screens, a View dropping the columns, Clear restoring sort / filters / pickers / filter row, owner save and delete; the real server — seeding (22 in 6 groups), guest read-only, anon 401, `cleanScreens` on junk, the list restored; `extremeAges` unit-checked; views, filter row, scores and advice suites unchanged.
- **An incident the same day: every screen lost its `<`/`>` filters in production.** `SCREEN_FILTER_VAL_RE` excluded `<` and `>`, which the filter grammar is made of (`>=3`, `<20`), so `cleanScreens()` silently dropped 13 screens' filters — and the local test's "restore the list" PUT went through that sanitizer against the production database, so the stored screens kept only their sorts and columns (Day gainers sorted everything by Today and filtered nothing). The stub tests passed because they fed `STARTER_SCREENS` to the page directly and never round-tripped it through the server. Fixed by allowing any printable value (everything is escaped where shown), a **round-trip check that `cleanScreens(STARTER_SCREENS)` keeps every filter**, and the 22 definitions written back to production. Lessons: a test that writes to production must restore through a path that cannot transform the data, and a sanitizer needs a round-trip test on the real definitions.
- **Found and fixed on the way**: the member Research menu still linked to the deleted `/signal` page, and **`/lab` has been broken since 2026-09-15's momentum cleanup** — `private/signal-stats.js` was deleted though `lab.html` calls `SignalStats.summarise/buckets/mean`; the file is restored from the commit before its deletion.

## Analysis screens (retired — kept for the thresholds' history)
**The seven predicates live in `private/screens.js`, not in the page.** `analysis.html` loads it with a `<script>` tag and `server.js` `require`s it, so the nightly report and the page can never disagree about what "bouncing off the lows" means — the same reason `rowcard.js` exists. Only the *selection* is shared (which rows, in what order); the columns, the prose and the empty messages stay with whichever surface is drawing them. Verified equivalent against the live universe on extraction: all seven lists identical, order included.

`/analysis` is seven filtered views built from the **raw fields, not the composite scores** — the scores already drive the table's ranking, and a screen that just re-sorts them adds nothing. Every threshold below was set by running the candidate against the live universe: a screen returning 0 names is a dead box, and one returning 25 of 69 is not a signal. **The hit counts in the table were measured at 69 symbols and the universe is now 116 — expect every one of them to be larger, and the 33 `Faded` names to crowd the bounce and value screens in particular.** Re-tune the thresholds against a fresh snapshot before reading anything into them.

| screen | rule | hits when set |
|---|---|---|
| Bouncing off the lows | `range52Pos ≤ 30` (or `pctFromHigh ≤ −25`), `1M > 3%`, `2W > 0` | 5 |
| Just started moving | `2W > 5%` while `3M < 0` | 2 |
| Drifting after a beat | surprise 10–100%, reported ≤ 21d | 2 |
| Reporting in 14 days | unchanged | 4 |
| Cheap, growing, profitable | fwd P/E < 20, rev > 15%, margin > 15% | 13 |
| Business improving, price isn't | rev > 25%, `3M < −10%` | 13 |
| Overextended | RSI > 75 and > 12% above the 50-day | 2 |

- **`range52Pos`** (0 = on the 52-week low, 100 = on the high) and **`pctFromLow`** are derived in `computeStocks()` from bars already fetched, so they cost no credits. Snapshots written before they existed fall back to `pctFromHigh ≤ −25`, and the section prints a note saying so. **The `≤ 30` threshold is unverified against real range data** — it was tuned on the fallback — so expect to adjust that one number after the first refresh.
- **Earnings surprises above 100% are excluded** as feed artefacts. Two different mega-caps currently report *exactly* +214%, which is a data bug rather than a coincidence; without the cap they would top the drift screen.
- **`netCash` is meaningless for financials** — if a balance-sheet screen is ever added, exclude the Financial Services sector, or JPM and HOOD will lead it.
- `analystConsensus`, `targetUpside` and the forward estimates are **0/69** on the current plan, so nothing can be built on them.
- Two removed screens ("Good business, price not working" / "Price working, business not") keyed off Quality and Momentum ratings; "Business improving, price isn't" is the same idea on raw revenue growth. "Deepest drawdowns" went too — it is the bounce screen's population without the part that matters, whether the thing has turned.

## API patterns
- `GET /api/stocks` — serves snapshot to public; `?refresh=1` recomputes live (admin only)
- `GET /api/stocks?asOf=YYYY-MM-DD` — the forward-returns view (admin only)
- `POST /api/refresh-all` — expires the profile cache, forces re-pull (admin only); `DELETE` ends the refresh flag
- `GET /api/status` — `{ refreshing }` only; polled by every open page during a refresh
- `POST /api/cron/refresh` — the nightly job's one round (bearer `CRON_SECRET`, not admin); `?start=1` begins a run, `DELETE` ends one early
- All portfolio/ticker CRUD routes require admin

## Conventions
- All persistence goes through `db.js` — never reintroduce `fs` reads/writes for state; a serverless filesystem discards them
- No build step — vanilla JS, plain CSS. The design system lives in `public/app.css`; each page keeps only its own layout in one inline `<style>` block
- Admin-only UI elements use class `admin-only` — toggled by `applyAdminUI()` in index.html
- **The CSV export was removed on 2026-09-14** (the owner had planned to retire it; it was the one bulk-copy button in the UI and fell with the watermark work). `esc()` — the escaper scoped inside it that twice caused bugs — went with it; `we()` is now the page's only escaper. Older notes in this file about CSV headers naming the weighting/horizon describe the removed feature.
